put your fangs in me 2

by villhag

Summary

The sequel to Put Your Fangs in Me.

Wanda doesn't think she'll find love. Agatha doesn't think she can raise a kid. Rio thinks something very evil has just arrived in New York City. They all might be correct.

Chapter 1

Wanda Maximoff was going to die alone.

That was, if she ever had the pleasure of dying.

Vampires were immortal by default, but some were more easily done away with than others. Unfortunately, Wanda was one of the frustratingly resilient ones. Practically unkillable. It was a complete disaster.

So she’d probably be cockroaching her way through time and space until the sun finally exploded.

Someone loudly cleared their throat. Wanda looked up, her doom increasing as she remembered what exactly had made her come to such a conclusion.

Sitting across from her was Man Von Whatshisface. Kidding. She had no idea what his name was. All she remembered from his VampireMatch dating profile was the “Von” part, because there was a small chance it meant he was also as ancient as she was.

It turned out to be a secret, worse, second thing.

The man was twenty-eight years old, five years into being a vampire, and he changed his last name because he thought it sounded more vampire-y.

“As I was saying,” he said. Wanda stared at the hairy caterpillar above his lip as he spoke. It looked almost alive. Fascinating. “I started as a stock trader right out of uni.”

“Uni?” Her eyes flicked up. “What the hell is uni?”

“University?”

“Ah,” she said, leaning back in her chair and taking a long sip of her coffee. “I think this date is done. That was the sixth time you abbreviated something that didn’t need to be abbreviated. I’m not particularly interested in someone who can’t finish the second half of a word.”

His eyes widened, his cheeks flushed, and he let out a sharp, disapproving laugh. The obvious misery in it was the first thing that gave Wanda joy all day.

“Jeez,” he said. “You’re a real—”

Wanda flashed him a great wide smile, and his tongue froze in his mouth. His pupils dilated. The hairs of his mustache stood straight up like he’d been electrocuted.

She perched her chin on her hand, bored, just watching it happen. The frustrating thing about Wanda’s power was that she could never see what they saw. She would have loved to know what this man’s greatest fear was. He was probably visualizing his Robinhood account getting hacked, or his stocks vesting a year late. Poor thing.

Either way, it ended as it usually did. Man Von Guy screamed at the top of his lungs, ran out of the shop like a headless chicken, and descended into the swell of hot dog carts, Tiktok- streaming street performers, overly vertical tour buses, and knockoff handbag scammers that made up the innards and organs of New York City in late July.

“Wow. Can you teach me how you do that?”

Wanda nearly came out of her seat. She’d been indulging in too much schadenfreude again; she’d been completely snuck up on.

She turned her head toward the sound of the light, charming voice. Despite her initial shock, that voice was like hearing doves sing compared to the nails-on-a-chalkboard conversation she’d been wrestled into minutes before.

Standing next to her table was a Barbie doll of a woman. Tall, impossibly blonde—in a way that adults should never be, it was unnerving—with her hair slicked back in a messy bun. She was, in a word, manicured.

She was also an employee. The apron she was wearing—much too unfashionable for this woman to be wearing otherwise—told her so.

“You’re new,” Wanda said, rather stupidly. She had been coming to the Nightingale everyday since she relocated to New York City. So every day for about… a year and a half.

“And you’re Wanda Maximoff.”

Wanda blinked. She looked down to see if she was wearing a name tag. She was not.

The woman laughed at her. It was, again, a very pleasant sound. Endearing, even. Which was very bad news for Wanda.

Because if the Nightingale had finally found a way to hire someone with an emotional IQ higher than 3, that meant the insect plague known as tourists would soon arrive at her blissfully dirty doorstep.

She enjoyed this coffee shop for a series of very specific reasons: the staff were rude, the coffees were overpriced, the bathroom sink was always broken, the pipes made a sound every five minutes that sounded like a bomb going off, the kitchen most likely had an unhandled mold problem, and, most of all, because it only entertained a customer every two and a half hours, due to previously mentioned reasons.

Having an attractive and non-offputting member of the staff meant one of those things might change. Wanda could not hide her horror. Thankfully she had the foresight to stop her power from activating as she grimaced. Even though it would have been a very practical solution to her problem, she didn’t actually like scaring people who didn’t deserve it.

The girl’s smile had dropped slightly. “Did I say something to offend you?”

Wanda shook her head. “No. It’s fine.” She sighed. “Why are you working here?”

Wanda thought this was a perfectly respectable question to ask. The girl didn’t seem to agree, because she laughed in her face.

“Is there a problem with me working here?”

She sounded a little offended. Wanda was offended on her behalf that she thought so little of herself.

“Of course there is. You—no offense, I’m sure there’s more to you than the fact that you’re an adult blonde, but it’s pretty distracting—but you look like an expensive collector’s edition doll. And your smile muscles still work. This place is a soul-sucking dump. Could you not get a job somewhere else? Like Starbucks? Or, if you really hate yourself, the Juice Palace?” Wanda said, matter-of-fact, then bit down on her tongue when she realized she’d forgotten to address something. “Wait, how do you know my name?”

The adult blonde stared at her for an agonizingly long moment. Wanda wasn’t sure why it was so agonizing. If she could do one thing in life, it was wait. She’d once waited for twenty- three days in a solitary confinement cell in the freezing cold of Siberia. That was what she got for relying on Agatha to get anything done in a timely fashion.

Anyway, patience was a virtue she possessed in spades. But currently, she felt unnerved. Her knee was bobbing. Maybe it was the bad date. Or the consistently bad coffee.

“Your reputation precedes you,” the woman confessed with a small smile. “There was a note on the wall of the kitchen when I arrived with your coffee order. A double espresso, topped with two tablespoons of hot water. Which is—”

“I refuse to call it an Americano,” Wanda cut in. “I will not indulge the belief that America invented anything. The country’s barely a toddler.”

“The post-it note mentioned that opinion, too.” The girl snorted. “You’re a lot.”

Wanda was genuinely struck by her honesty. It made her laugh, genuinely laugh; it also made her knee bob a little faster. “Thank you. I am. That’s how I do it, by the way. The driving away men bit. By being a lot. If you really wanted to take notes.”

“Thank you, I’ll remember that,” she said, then tapped her finger—french tip pink nails, so Barbie—to Wanda’s notebook. Well, one of her notebooks. There were around fifteen in total stacked on top of each other like Jenga. Several had coffee stains from their various interactions with the floor. “You seem to take a lot of notes. Student?”

Wanda blinked incredulously. “Do I look that young to you?”

“Well, you certainly don’t look old.”

Wanda would have laughed, but for some reason it stung a bit. There was a certain loneliness in not looking your age. No wrinkles to bear all the things you’d endured. God, I sound like a character from a soap opera. Being immortal was both incredibly isolating and also totally silly. Sometimes it hurt to become aware of it, sometimes it didn’t.

“Take whatever age you’re thinking about, then multiply it by a thousand,” she said, holding the woman’s gaze for a moment before turning back to her work. Her pen had lapsed next to an unsolved equation. She usually took her dates during her breaks. Quantum equations had so far been a lot more tantalizing than anyone she’d met.

Instead of disappearing like Wanda would expect her to, the woman just stood there, put a hand on her hip, and hummed.

“Huh. So that would put you right at the death of Charlemagne.”

Before Wanda thought about it, she reflexively replied, “Fifteen years after, actually, not that I can remember much—” She froze; realized the girl was joking.

Of course she was joking. This was a human barista at fucking Nightingale. She looked all of twenty-seven years old. Maybe twenty-eight.

But what the hell? Why had she been so dead on?

Wanda’s whole body suddenly itched. Itched to leave; itched to say something. The woman bit down on her lip and looked like she was about to say something when a man in an ashtray of an outfit stepped out from the kitchen.

“Bella!” he said, waving towards them. “Come here! Chad just got a five dollar lottery ticket! He needs you to run to the gas station to redeem it. He says you can buy yourself something nice with a dollar fifty of the winnings, eh? What does that get you these days?”

The woman, this completely baffling woman—Bella?—kept her gaze pinned on Wanda for a moment longer before she sighed, turned on her heel, and addressed the owner of this fine dining establishment. “Absolutely nothing, Larry. Nothing.”

Before she departed, though, she turned back once more, and gently touched Wanda on the hand. “It was nice to meet you, Wanda,” she said. “And I’m eager to hear more about what it was like living in the Holy Roman Empire next time we chat.”

Wanda did nothing but blink at her. She would have said something, anything, really, but Bella was headed to the bar and Wanda’s phone was vibrating so loudly it nearly toppled off the table. She caught it at the last second, taking a breath in before she noticed who was lighting up the screen.

Financial Leech: so, how’d the date go? This one a keeper, or did you just do calculus on the side like last time, you complete nerd?

Financial Leech: also, asking for a friend, what the fuck are you supposed to do if your child has started levitating out of their crib at night

Chapter 2

Chapter Notes

In which Wanda pays a visit to her financial leech.

“Huh. You really weren’t kidding.”

If there was one certainty in Wanda’s life, it was that she could rely on Agatha Harkness for nothing. Least of all the truth. But as she shucked off her coat and strolled into the Harkness/Vidal residence, a rent-controlled apartment on the Upper East Side, where the rent was controlled, of course, by Wanda, there really was a levitating baby.

The pregnancy had come as a serious surprise to both Agatha and Rio. Not to Wanda, of course, because if Agatha had chosen to consult her for all of five minutes she could have supplied her with a field guide on vampire procreation—and informed her that it wasn’t something that was exclusive to those of the heterosexual persuasion, even though Catrina had always insisted that. No, on the contrary, after the marriage bite, any two vampires who stayed in close proximity long enough would end up with a baby.

It was how the species kept its lineage going.

Of course, that was not how Wanda ended up with Billy. She’d had Billy with a human. Never knew the name of the father. Didn’t want to know. She found him on a sperm donation site and sorted by highest degree of education.

It was quick and easy, too easy; she’d wanted a kid, and she’d pretty much given up on finding a vampire partner to have one with, so she went with a human instead. It was a spur of the moment decision. One that gave her the greatest joy of her life—her Billy—but also the greatest regret in her life—her Billy.

Because the thing with a human is—they eventually die.

No matter how much you love them.

Stop it. He’s alive. He just texted you from Taco Bell.

She ground her teeth together and shook off the thought. It was not difficult to do, as the present moment was an incredibly good distraction—Agatha had climbed onto the sofa and was now stretching both arms up towards the ceiling, miserably failing at capturing the giggling child that was crawling across the white plaster.

“Why would I joke about something like this?” Agatha snapped at Wanda. Then her voice lowered to something pleading and almost maternal as she beckoned the infant to come off the ceiling. “Nicholas. Please. Your mother has not had her coffee yet today and she’s trying to break a generational cycle of hating one’s child.”

Wanda was about to ask where Rio had gone when the woman of the hour materialized in nothing but a Metallica t-shirt and black underwear from the bathroom, a toothbrush hanging out of her mouth. Even though Wanda had been visiting them for over a year now, it still startled her to see the Murder Princess herself reduced to an outfit from Uniqlo. Their whole domestic bliss thing was just… strange.

Not that it was exactly domestic. Or bliss. The apartment was a complete mess. Agatha’s DVD collection had been moved into the living room to make space for Nicky’s future bedroom. The collection of discs was so large that they were having to repurpose some of them as footstools and side tables. Currently, Wanda was sitting in an armchair with her feet propped up on a collector’s edition Jaws Bluray.

“Oh hey there, Dracula, you’re early," Rio said, smirking as she stepped over season one of Friends and season two of The Wire to make her way to the couch. “Agatha, let me.”

Rio leaned over toward the kitchen table and grabbed a knocked over milk bottle. The tiny plastic thing was full of a red sloshing liquid.

“Absolutely not. You already fed him this morning and last night. I’m not letting you become the favorite parent this early in the game.”

Rio laughed, smiling grandly at her girlfriend. “You say that like it’s some kind of inevitability.”

Agatha scoffed. “It is. One, I’m so emotionally abrasive that I put you in a partial memory coma for six hundred years, and two, I’ll inevitably push him too hard to be good at something and you’ll be like aw Nicky, it’s fine, you just be whoever you are.”

“Well, we can get ahead of that. What if instead I push him really hard academically and you tell him he can be whoever he wants to be?”

Agatha made an unenthused noise. “You and your solutions. Give me that.”

Rio handed over the bottle with a laugh, and Agatha managed to wrangle the crocodile of an infant back into her arms. Once he was no longer defying gravity, she handed him over to Rio, and said, “Alright. Now you can be the good Mom and go change him.”

Rio sniffed the baby and grimaced. “I’d rather be the bad mother.”

“Sorry. Too bad. That slot’s already taken. Get on with it so I can find out if Wanda met the love of her life yesterday. I’m dying with anticipation.”

Wanda rolled her eyes. “The sarcasm is not lost on me.”

“That’s a first,” Agatha snickered.

Rio swatted her girlfriend on the shoulder, muttered “Be nice, she pays our rent,” and then made her way to Nicky’s room, nearly tripping over the vacuum cleaner that had been left splayed on the floor since the last time Wanda visited, which was four days ago.

“So?” Agatha made an expectant gesture with her hands. “Tell me everything.”

Wanda sighed. For some strange reason, she felt uncomfortable discussing it. She’d found it easy to go on and on about how terrible her previous twenty dates had gone, but this one had left a boulder in her stomach. It was weird.

“Nothing to tell,” she said. “Total dud.”

“Aw, really?” Agatha fake-pouted; batted her eyelashes. “And who could have told you that? Someone really wise, probably.”

This woman. “Yes, the one who accidentally got herself a vampire baby because she didn’t consult me first is the wise one in this equation.”

“I didn’t realize you were a vampire sex expert,” Agatha groaned. “You had a kid with a human! Anyone can have a kid with a human.”

“The identity of my child’s father has no bearing on my wiseness.”

Agatha shook her head, groaning again, and Wanda found herself smiling. She couldn’t stand Agatha most of the time—she was chronically late, rude, and of course the money thing—but about twenty percent of the time she made her feel just… good. At ease.

It was a little bit like having a twin. You didn’t have to talk about anything of substance with them in order to feel comforted. Being as old as they were meant that no matter where you had lived on Earth, you had a semblance of a shared history—you’d endured the same plagues and empires and politics and revolutions and deaths and births. All of that went unsaid, but it was still important. You couldn’t find that in a human.

“So that’s it? Nothing more to tell?” Agatha said. “God, you’re boring. Come on, give me some kind of fun detail. Was he wearing an eyepatch even though he had two functioning eyes? Did he try to get you to join his multi-level marketing scheme?”

“No, and no. He was just a man,” Wanda said, blowing a red strand of hair out of her eyes. “The barista was much more interesting, honestly.”

Agatha’s eyes flashed with that wolfish curiosity and Wanda immediately regretted her choice of words.

“Not like that. She’s a human. A Barbie-shaped human being.”

“She!” Agatha clapped her hands. “At least we’re getting somewhere.”

Wanda rolled her eyes. “You know I date both.”

“Ah! So you’re admitting you want to date her!” Agatha giggled. The woman actually giggled. Then she shouted loudly, so Rio could hear, “Wanda’s dating a human woman!”

Rio’s head immediately materialized by the doorframe. There were bags under her eyes that weren’t there five minutes ago. Apparently, changing the diaper of a baby who can levitate presents its own challenges. “You’re dating a human?”

“A woman human,” Agatha reiterated.

“I’m not dating her! Or anyone!” Wanda covered her face in humiliation. “And honestly I think I’m going to stop trying.” Agatha opened her mouth; Wanda cut her off. “I know you think it’s good for me. But I moved here for my work. For my research. For my new position at Columbia. Everything else is just a distraction.”

“Finding love is a distraction from… doing math?” Rio asked, rocking the now sleeping Nicholas back and forth in her arms as she rejoined Agatha on the couch.

“Well, yes!” she huffed. “When that math has the potential to be revolutionary in the field of health and disease science, then absolutely. I am the premier biophysicist in the country. If I don’t spend my time on these theories, then who will? Human lifetimes are short. If I can make them even a little bit longer, then I need to do that now, not later.”

Wanda’s fingers curled into fists, her chest trembling. Embarrassing. You’re embarrassing yourself. Rio bit her lip guiltily; Agatha looked at her like she was watching a bomb go off. Great, you did it again. Wanda knew they were only teasing her, but it was very easy for her to get worked up about the subject. It was a soft spot; she knew it was. But she couldn’t hide her conviction. Outside of Billy, her conviction was all she had in this life.

“I know,” Agatha said softly. The unspoken drifted between them. Billy. If she could make Billy’s life a little bit longer, that would mean everything.

Silence enveloped the room. The wind whisked loudly against the windows.

“Well,” Wanda cleared her throat; anxiously clapped her hands against her pants. “This was fun. As always. But I think I’ll be going now.”

“Wanda, wait—”

Wanda turned on her heel, stepped over a skyscraper of Gilmore Girls DVDs, and let the door to the apartment fall flat behind her.

They didn’t need to understand. If Wanda was the only vampire on Earth who was going to give a damn about the future of humanity, then she’d be alone in that. And that was fine. Not all paths were walked with other people. Some were walked—

Wanda froze. She had been halfway to her apartment, treading down some alleyway parallel to the train station, lost in thought, when she realized she wasn’t near her apartment at all. Her stupid betraying feet had taken her to the Nightingale. Never mind the fact that it was nine pm and the coffee shop closed four hours ago. Completely unhelpful—she couldn’t even get herself a coffee. Well, she could always break in, but…

She wasn’t Agatha.

That was practically her life’s mantra at this point.

She found herself staring into the windows of the shop, expecting to see nothing inside except the grim, molding interior. But something was there. If she was a human, she wouldn’t have caught it. But vampire eyes are excellent for seeing in the dark. And through those exceptional pupils of hers, Wanda saw a woman lying on the floor.

A blonde woman. Tucked into the fetal position, sleeping. On the floor of the Nightingale. She would have assumed she was dead, but she could faintly hear her pulse through the shop’s paper-thin walls. She was snoring slightly, her nose scrunching.

That was Bella, wasn’t it? The new barista?

What the hell?

Wanda bit down on her lip. Walk away. Mind your business. Walk away. Mind your business.

Fuck. No, she needed to know.

She headed for the front door.

Chapter 3

Chapter Summary

The one where Wanda invites a stranger to live with her

How the hell do you break into a building in a normal way?

Wanda groaned. Being a vampire was like walking around the Museum of Glass with hammers for hands.

Vampiric powers made it easy to get things done—things like breaking someone’s neck, or persuading the president of a university into giving you millions of dollars in funding on a moonshot research project—but doing small things, quietly and without force, was much harder.

So she didn’t bother.

If anything, a broken door would help the Nightingale maintain its reputation.

The door came politely off its hinges with a quick yank. Wanda was no Rio, but she could get the job done. She didn’t let it fall to the ground—she didn’t want to startle Bella—so she just let it gently rest on the doorframe as she pushed past and into the shop.

She took several steps inside before she realized just how insane this was. What was her game plan, exactly? Wake the girl and say hey, I have no idea why you’re sleeping on a floor that is quite literally inventing new diseases as we speak, but I just wanted to let you know I could give you some money for a hotel if you wanted? Or if not a hotel, how about one of the fifteen places I own? You could even stay at Agatha’s apartment, I’ll throw them out without a second thought…

“Wanda?”

Wanda jumped. In a flash, Bella’s eyes had snapped open, and she was suddenly sitting up. How did she do that so fast? This is what Wanda got for forcing herself to blink every two seconds like humans did.

She’d taught herself the skill back when Billy started preschool, so she didn’t raise suspicions with the human parents, but now she still couldn’t shake the bad habit. It made her feel like she was missing frames in a movie.

Bella narrowed her eyes into the dark as she nervously combed her hand through her blonde hair; it was considerably more disheveled now, and Wanda thought she preferred it that way. Why do you care what state her hair is in? There were more pressing issues. The woman looked exhausted and anxious; of course she was nervous, she was probably homeless, and now she was worried Wanda was going to report her to her boss. Or the police. Or some other useless human authority that Agatha was constantly in the business of depopulating.

“Hi. Yes. Hello,” Wanda mumbled. She sucked in a breath to balance herself. “I’m sorry to startle you. It’s just that I was walking home… Well, walking with the intention to go home, but my feet took me entirely in the wrong direction… and I came across you sleeping on the floor and I thought to myself, that looks uncomfortable.”

Bella stared at her for a moment with a disquieting intensity. Seeing her now at night, without any makeup or customer service artifice, Wanda noticed for the first time that Bella had the eyes of a snowy owl. Black with a slightly yellow tint, and fiercely intense.

She’d never seen those kind of eyes on a human before. Or a vampire, for that matter. During most hours of the day, vampires had remarkably human eyes—green, blue, brown, all the traditional colors—except for when their powers activated, and they shaded red. Bella’s irises were a different matter entirely. Maybe it was a medical condition?

Whatever it was, it was almost intimidating.

Key word: almost. It was hard to intimidate someone who lived in London during the Black Death. Wanda had seen more bodies than most people have taken breaths.

“Uh huh,” Bella said. That intensity in her eyes slowly dissolved, as if she’d finished her assessment. “So you decided to break into the store and what, find me a blanket?”

“I—” Wanda stuttered. Her cheeks flushed the color of her hair. “I was simply concerned for your health.”

She must have looked completely embarrassed with herself, because the blonde laughed and shook her head.

“Oh?” Bella laughed. “It’s sweet of you to worry. I’m just teasing you.”

Wanda blinked at her. “You’re… teasing me.”

“Has no one ever done that before?”

“Certainly not from the mold-infested floor of my local coffee place,” Wanda said, trying to find her conversational footing again. She was ancient, for God’s sake. This woman was barely in existence.

Bella cracked another grin at her joke, then sighed wistfully. “It’s not actually mold-infested, unfortunately.”

The blonde rose from the ground without using an ounce of strength. This woman moved so fluidly, so quickly—she had to be a former ballerina. She certainly fit the profile.

“Unfortunately?” Wanda questioned as Bella walked herself to the counter. A small black, heart-shaped purse was sitting there. “That would be fortunate to you?”

Bella rifled through the purse for a moment before withdrawing a lipstick, uncapping it, and smudging it across her lips. The color was a dark, almost muddy red. Like dried blood.

Why is she putting on lipstick at nine pm?

Ignoring Wanda’s question, Bella said instead, “I’m surprised you were able to spot me from the sidewalk. I double-checked earlier if you can see the interior from the outside, and you can’t. Are you sure you don’t have superpowers?”

She swiped a last stroke across her upper lip then set it aside. Wanda swallowed.

To avoid getting into a discussion about the peculiarities of her vision, she pivoted, “Bella, I don’t mean to pry, but do you not have somewhere to live?”

Bella gave her a cat-like smile. “Do you think I’m sleeping here for fun?”

“This is New York City,” Wanda huffed. “People do stranger things.”

“Fair enough.” Bella shrugged, then leaned against the counter, all-too-casual for a woman who’d just been curled up on the floor. “Technically I have a place, but my roommate is awful. She’s one of those obsessively cleanly types, so the room always smells like bleach and vinegar. I think it was burning holes inside of my nose.”

A dormitory? “Wait,” Wanda said, dread stirring inside of her. “You’re a student?”

Bella plucked something else out of her purse. It was rectangular. A card? No. Wanda instantly recognized the emblem. It was a student badge. A Columbia University student badge.

She wordlessly handed it over to Wanda, and Wanda stared at it in the dark.

Bella Dragomir. Department of Biological Sciences.

Dragomir, was her first thought. Sounds Romanian.

Her second thought was:

“Oh god,” Wanda felt nauseous. It was mortifying enough to be half-stalking a random woman, but the random woman was a student at her university? Worse than that, in her department? It was truly a misfortune that Wanda couldn’t die. “Please don’t tell me you’re an undergraduate.”

Bella laughed, looking positively delighted by the accusation. “No, but thank you for thinking so,” she said, biting down on her lip. “Post-doc. Studying mycology.”

She’s not a student at all. Relief washed over Wanda, and she wasn’t particularly sure why. She told herself it was because she couldn’t stand undergraduates. They were somehow even more ignorant than high schoolers.

Feeling in her element, Wanda nodded. “Mycology. So that’s why you’re weirdly into mold,” she said, then added, “And against bleach.”

“Guilty.”

They just studied each-other for a moment. Wanda felt something stir in her stomach. It was probably the stench of the place. They had clearly left today’s trash to rot somewhere in the kitchen. Wanda couldn’t imagine sleeping in these kinds of conditions.

Wanda swallowed. What she wanted to say was If you need a place to live, I can give you one but that felt strangely forward so she settled on, “Why did you just put lipstick on?”

Bella blinked. For the first time since Wanda snuck up on her in the dead of night, she looked actually caught off guard. She touched her finger to her lip and made an o shape with her mouth when it came away red, as if she’d surprised herself.

“Second nature, I suppose,” she replied. “It’s the first thing I do after I wake up in the morning. Have to put my face on.”

“But it’s nine pm. Where’s your face going?”

Bella let out another sharp laugh. Wanda relished in it. “Well, I guess my clock’s off,” Bella drawled, stepping forward into Wanda’s personal space and grinning. “Given that some random customer decided to break into the shop and wake me up. Hm?”

Wanda pinched the bridge of her nose, flushing deeper. “Ah—yes. Right. I’m sorry.”

“It’s fine. I’m a terrible sleeper anyways. Now that I’m up, I’m up,” Bella laughed, withdrawing from Wanda’s bubble. Wanda felt a strange sensation in her gut at the sudden loss of proximity. “So, you want me to make you a coffee?”

Wanda’s mouth opened, then closed. “A coffee? It’s nine pm.”

Of course, she would really have loved a coffee. The days she wasn’t teaching, she slept vampire hours, meaning her waking hours were typically 4pm-10am. A nine pm coffee was by no means outrageous. But she couldn’t raise suspicions over something so silly.

“I can make it decaf,” Bella offered.

Wanda let out a shrill laugh. “Decaf is an insult to coffee.”

“Thought you’d say that,” Bella huffed. Despite Wanda’s protests, Bella squatted down and checked that the espresso machine was still plugged in. Wanda frowned at the mystery liquid sitting dangerously close to the electrical socket. “So, are you going to tell my boss that I’m camping out?”

Wanda’s pulse picked up at the abrupt question. Bella didn’t even look at her when she asked it; her pale hands were busy plucking a mug from the sink and scrubbing it clean. Wanda was surprised the streetlights outside gave her enough illumination to see what she was doing, but she seemed completely comfortable.

“Of course not,” Wanda said. “I would have nothing to gain by doing that.”

“Good,” Bella replied. “I was hoping I wouldn’t have to threaten you.”

Wanda let out a noise of disbelief. The other woman was smiling to herself, as if she had anticipated Wanda’s reaction already. “I’m sorry. Threaten me?”

Bella set down the cup, grabbed a napkin, and began to dry it off. She was meticulous with her motions. Precise and surgical, with steady hands. Clearly she made a good lab tech.

After it was dry, she wordlessly filled it up with an Americano, then leaned over the counter and innocently offered her the steaming hot beverage.

“Of course,” Bella said, letting out a slow breath. Wanda stared at the drink, then flicked her eyes upward to meet Bella’s. “It wouldn’t be hard to blackmail you.”

Completely enraptured by the turn of this conversation, Wanda saw no other option other than to take the drink. Sue her. It smelled tantalizing.

“And how would you go about doing that?” Wanda said, taking a molten hot sip.

“Don’t burn yourself.”

“I’ve already melted off all the nerve endings,” Wanda said, not missing a beat. “Get on with it. How would you blackmail me?”

Bella propped her elbows on the counter and tapped her chin thoughtfully.

“I’d probably start with sending a message to your dean.”

Wanda blinked, taken aback. “I thought you knew my name because of my order.”

“I do know your name because of your order,” Bella said, turning her head to the side like a dog listening for a command. “I also know it because you’re the winner of three early-career research awards, former principal investigator on two NIH-funded projects, and the youngest faculty hire in your department’s history. I also know that you engineered a fake scandal for your predecessor that allowed you to get this job.”

At that last comment, Wanda’s fingertips trembled. She let out a trapped, stilted breath. She could not remember a time in modern history that a human had made her sweat.

“There is no evidence of that,” she whispered. There really wasn’t. She’d had Agatha trance the guy into stripping naked in the quad, completely of his own volition. Well, of Agatha’s volition, but still. No human would be able to tell. “Who the hell are you?”

Bella smiled grandly. Her teeth were eerily perfect. White as ivory.

“I’m just someone who likes to do their research.” Wanda wasn’t sure when their faces had gotten so close together. She was grateful for the mug sitting between her nose and Bella’s, as it provided the singular barricade preventing them from—from what? It’s not like Wanda was going to— “How’s the coffee taste?”

Wanda almost choked on it.

“Unfortunately,” she muttered. “Spectacular.”

“Good. Want another? On the house.”

“No. Of course not. And I insist on paying.”

“You’re no fun.”

“And you’re insane. I might just have to get you fired.”

Bella laughed, tilting her head back so her long blonde hair was nearly at her waist.

“I think you’d regret that,” she said.

“Probably,” Wanda replied, then licked her lips. Don’t say it. Don’t say it. “Would you like to live with me?”

Chapter 4

Chapter Summary

In which billionaire vampire Wanda Maximoff realizes not everyone else has unlimited funds (and also, that she might have a crush on a human)

“Live… with you?”

Idiot. Idiot. Idiot.

Why had she said that? She’d meant to say literally anything except that. Wanda had five apartments within Manhattan alone. She could have offered any one of them.

Then again, it wasn’t necessarily a good idea to flaunt her exorbitant wealth to a coworker. As far as the university (and her US tax returns) were concerned, she was upper middle class at most. The only income she registered was the salary she got from Columbia and a handful of stocks of bonds that seemed believable for a professor of her age.

Of course, Wanda possessed a lot more than a handful of stocks and bonds. She would easily rank in the top ten of richest people on planet Earth, and hiding that was no simple task. She had a long list of shell companies and property managers that took care of all her estates and business interests (and more importantly, heavily smudged her paper trail.) She had enough accountants that she had an assistant who just dealt with her accountants. And then an assistant who dealt with that assistant.

Her entire life was designed so that she could do exactly what she wanted to do with the least interference from other people as possible. That kind of lifestyle required discretion. She had no interest in disassembling it one afternoon.

So Wanda cleared her throat, and doubled down. “Sure. I have an extra room. Three of them, actually. So you can have your pick of the litter. I see no reason that one of my colleagues should be sleeping on the floor of a coffee shop while I have the space.”

“Would the university staff not find that strange?”

Wanda bit down on her lip. What she really wanted to say was if any of them did find it strange, I would find zero problem in getting Agatha to Suggest them into taking a long vacation, but that didn’t seem prudent. So she became the personification of class and dignity instead, propping her hands on her hips and nodded once, solemnly.

“That’s true,” she said, feigning concern. “I have a solution, though. We’ll just not tell them.” At that, Bella full-body laughed. Her head careened forward, her long hair splaying over the countertop. It looked like a blonde waterfall.

“I can see why they hired you,” Bella said, tipping her chin back up to look at Wanda. “You truly operate on a higher level.”

To Wanda’s great horror, she found herself smiling. “Oh, fuck you.”

Bella’s lips spread into a cartoonish frown, and she slowly leaned away from the counter. Wanda thought for a moment she might have actually injured the girl’s ego, but then the blonde circled around the countertop, slung her tiny black purse over her shoulder, and gestured toward the door.

“Lead the way.”

Wanda blinked. Then, realizing Bella was actually agreeing to her ludicrous plan, she panicked. Internally, of course. Externally, it probably manifested as a singular eye twitch.

It’s fine. You’re just being charitable. You’re always being charitable.

After all, with the amount of money she gave away to Agatha every month, she was practically Mother Teresa.

***

“Professor Maximoff, this isn’t an apartment. This is a mansion.”

“If you ever call me that again, I will evict you on the spot.”

Bella snickered, closing the door and shutting out the incessant honking. It had been a brisk —and awkward—fifteen minute walk north to the Upper West Side, where Wanda’s home sat across from “Central Park.” Or, as Wanda liked to call it, “Pissing Field For Dogs.”

She had a lot of opinions about twenty-first century dog ownership.

“What should I call you then?” Bella asked.

“My name,” Wanda said, removing her leather boots and whisking open the coat closet. Distracted by the paintings lining the walls, Bella began to step into the foyer, but Wanda stopped her with a snappy, “No shoes in the house.”

Bella immediately froze up. “Sorry.”

Wanda grimaced. Way to go. You probably sound like her mother. “No. It’s fine. I’m—I’m just weird about germs. Outside shoes, inside shoes. You know.”

Bella kicked off her shoes. “You’re weird about germs and yet you choose to hang out at the coffee shop in Manhattan with possibly the worst kitchen hygiene I’ve ever seen?” Wanda winced. That excuse usually worked, but this girl was annoyingly keen. The truth was, she didn’t care about germs in the least. She was a vampire, meaning her gut biome was built out of steel and spikes, and even the most deadly human diseases usually manifested in some bizarre but totally harmless side effect.

For example, when she caught the Black Death, she spent the whole week with a horrible itch between her shoulder blades that she couldn’t scratch.

She was just very… particular about her home. Which was why, on days where she wasn’t acting like a complete lunatic, she did not invite people over. It was easier that way.

“Forget about it,” Wanda said, shaking her head and taking Bella’s shoes from her. They were heavier than Wanda expected—the pointy kitten heel was as weighty as a boulder—and she dropped them onto the carpet. “Damn it.”

She kneeled down to grab them, and Bella apparently had the same idea. Wanda’s hand fell over Bella’s as it wrapped around the shoe.

Wanda froze. Her first instinct was to release it and apologize, but the feel of Bella’s hand was strangely distracting: her skin was abnormally warm, and softer than even the rare silks Wanda had kept through the centuries. It almost didn’t feel like normal skin. It felt like she was made of mink.

God, I wouldn’t mind a blanket made out of her.

“Wanda?”

Wanda’s eyes snapped up. Bella looked confused, perhaps mildly startled.

Please dear heavenly father I pray I didn’t say that out loud.

“Yes?" Wanda said, her voice strained.

“You’re gripping my hand a bit tightly.”

Wanda turned the shade of red she expected the sun might turn when it eventually exploded. She didn’t even try to apologize; she just released the woman’s hand, turned towards the dark closet, and pinched the bridge of her nose until the shame lessened.

After a moment, she released a breath. “It’s late. Must be after ten or eleven now,” she said, as if that might explain her behavior. “Let me show you to your room.”

Without glancing back, she led Bella into the living room, then up the spiral staircase onto the second floor. Thankfully Bella couldn’t have been overly affected by the interaction, because she was busy marveling at just about everything in Wanda’s apartment. Which, Wanda would admit, gave her a modicum of satisfaction. It was so rare she had guests over.

“I take back what I said before,” Bella said as they made their way onto the second floor landing. “This isn’t a mansion. This is a museum. You have so much… everything.” “I’m a bit of a collector,” Wanda said quietly. Every vampire was.

Wanda didn’t realize Bella was no longer following her until she found herself talking to the hallway wall. She looked behind to find the blonde holding a picture frame.

She turned it in Wanda’s direction, arching an eyebrow expectantly.

Wanda’s stomach fell. It was Billy. A picture of them at his college graduation.

No one at Columbia knew about Billy. Even during the fiasco of Tommy’s death, she’d managed to keep Billy out of the public eye. He’d always gone to local schools in Albany, nothing private, nothing fancy. Outside of Agatha and Rio, almost no one else in the mortal or the vampire world knew he was her son. He was safer that way.

“My nephew,” Wanda answered.

“Oh,” Bella said, squinting at it. “Makes sense. His hair is nowhere near as cool as yours.”

Wanda laughed in shock. “As cool? You find my hair cool?”

“Well, I find you cool in general,” Bella said, winking in a way that made Wanda feel suddenly very nervous. The blonde set down the picture and walked toward Wanda, joining her by the doorframe to the bedroom. “But the phoenix hair really sells it.”

“Er… thank you,” Wanda said unsteadily, because Bella was very close again and Wanda was scared she was going to think something even worse than that blanket thought, although it seemed impossible to imagine what that could be. “So, your room.”

“My room,” Bella echoed, mercifully shifting her gaze from Wanda to their surroundings. Her voice was oddly quiet when she continued, “Feels strange to say that.”

“Have you… not had a room of your own before?”

Bella turned her head back towards Wanda. She was wearing a small smile, one that didn’t reach her eyes. Hardly her usual exuberance.

“Always shared with my sisters.”

“Oh,” Wanda said. She realized in that moment just how little she knew about the stranger she’d invited into her house. “You have sisters.”

Bella laughed, and Wanda felt a warmth in her stomach seeing how free it looked, her usual impishness returning color to her face.

“As many people do,” Bella joked.

Wanda rolled her eyes. “Not me. Only a brother.”

“Ah. My condolences.”

“Thank you. He is dead though, actually. Fun fact.”

Bella’s face fell catastrophically. “I’m so sorry. I didn’t mean…”

“It’s fine.” It’s been a thousand years. “It’s been a while.”

Bella frowned at her, and Wanda decided she didn’t like that expression on her, so she tugged Bella by the arm, dragging her out of the infernally claustrophobic doorframe and into the bedroom. Then she closed the door shut behind them out of reflex. (Back before the miracle of central heating, doors needed to always be tightly closed to keep the heat in.)

Bella apparently did not understand this reflex, because her eyes widened and she laughed again—at least the frown’s gone. “Wanda, what are you doing?”

“What do you mean what am I doing?”

“Closing the door to the bedroom?” Bella said. “Is this going to be a sleepover?”

Wanda spluttered. “Of course not. I have a bedroom.”

“Oh. That’s disappointing.”

“What?” It is?

Bella seemed to not care that she was leaving Wanda in distress, because she proceeded to open the dresser drawers, innocently peering inside.

“These are empty,” Bella astutely observed.

“Well, of course they are. Why would I put stuff in the drawers of a guest room?”

“I guess it’s just hard for me to imagine the existence of a guest room. My two sisters and I were constantly warring over closet space.”

Wanda’s mouth opened, then closed. It’d been so many years since she’d ever had to think about money, she often forgot just how little others lived with.

“Do your sisters live in New York as well?”

Bella slowly shut the dresser drawers, pressing her hands flush to the oak.

“No,” she said, without any emotion. “They’re still back at home.”

“Home, okay. Which is… where?”

Avoiding the question, Bella just flashed her a smile. Then, to Wanda’s horror, she curled her warm hands around Wanda’s arms.

“Thank you so much for letting me stay the night, Wanda,” she said, in a quiet voice that made the hairs on Wanda’s arms stand straight. “And don’t worry, I’ll be out of your hair before you even wake up. I have to teach a class at eight-thirty, and I’ll have to get back to my office by six so I can pick up my lecture materials and a change of clothes.”

“You keep your clothes… at your office?”

Bella shrugged. “I keep everything there. I only make trips to my apartment when I have to. Dealing with my roommate is like stepping into a lion’s den.”

“You should find a new roommate,” Wanda said, her voice trembling embarrassingly.

It was just—she couldn’t help it—this woman’s hands were like portable ovens, and her pupils were fully dilated, and her hair was glowing under the low light of the oil lamp like some kind of fallen angel and God she was just annoyingly pretty.

Oh.

Hm.

She could not tell Agatha about this.

“I think I already found one,” Bella joked, curling her fingers into the flesh of Wanda’s arm again before releasing them. “You know, you should come to one of my lectures sometime. I wouldn’t say our areas of focus overlap much, but I’d be honored to have you attend.”

Wanda swallowed down the realization she’d just had, and the lengthy spiral of thoughts that immediately followed it.

“Of course. I have to make sure I’m not hosting a bad scientist.”

Bella snorted.

“I’m a lot of things, but not that,” she said, looking at Wanda with a glint in her eye “Goodnight Wanda. Don’t get lost on the way to your room.”

Wanda inhaled, and thought to herself—

You’ve made a really poor decision.

But all she said was, “Shut up.”

Chapter 5

Chapter Summary

The one where Wanda admits to herself that she’s down bad

It was not often that Wanda dreamt.

At least, not since she was a child.

Back then, when she was young and emotionally frail, it was very difficult for her to control her powers. Constant visions kept her submerged; she lived more of her life in her mind than she ever did in her body.

Her eyelids would fly open in the night, and she’d find her tongue dry from screaming, the bed wet with sweat. Burnt into her corneas would be the fading image of some war general’s decapitated head lolling on the ground.

The dreams were usually about war. Armies marching. Young delusional men forking each- other through the chest.

These were terribly unuseful topics to her, but very interesting to monarchs.

Subsequently, most of her money in the middle ages came from selling these premonitions to court sages and other fake-mystics, so that they could present her visions as proof of their own divinity. She watched a lot of false gods rise and fall while she sweated her sheets.

But by her two-hundredth birthday, she’d had a breakthrough. She’d come to understand the true nature of her powers. Well, perhaps not the true nature—there was still much she still did not know, even now—but she’d gained enough knowledge by then to realize that she was not actually a Divination vampire. Her dreams sometimes came true, sure, but that was only because she kept up with the times, and she was good at predicting how dominoes tended to fall. Much better than most of the tacticians the King employed.

In actuality, the reality was rather embarrassing; all Wanda was really doing was giving herself nightmares. Unknowingly pickpocketing fears from the heads of peasants and noblemen and casting those terrors into elaborate dramas in her own head.

A Nightmare vampire. There was no such thing in the traditional literature, so she had to name herself.

Not a particularly powerful skill, nightmares, or so she thought at the time. Of course, she had been dead wrong.

Fear, it turns out, is often a much more potent weapon than violence.

Regardless, she was able to stop projecting the nightmares inward after that. The visions stopped. She hardly dreamed at all for the next eight hundred years.

Which was why it was exceptionally odd that she found herself doing it now.

It came in a short, horrible burst just before her alarm. In her mind, she was in some sort of darkened closet, the door locked, so she had no way of leaving. Long ballgowns surrounded her, hung from handcrafted oak hangers. The dresses looked worse for wear, clearly unworn for decades; the whole room choked with dust and mothballs.

A powerful and impatient knock came at the closet door, startling her. A striking fear possessed Wanda just at the mere sound of it. Her whole body trembled like it was remembering something, but Wanda could not recall it.

“Can I leave now?” Wanda found herself saying. She’d had this sensation many times in her younger visions—words filling her mouth that she didn’t recognize, like a puppet.

“No,” answered the voice. It was old and feminine. Almost comforting in its tone, or it would have been, if it wasn’t coming from the other side of the door. “Two more years.”

Wanda’s heart lurched in her chest.

Years? Spent in here?

Everything narrowed. The ballgowns seemed to grow in size and press inward, their previously soft silky fabric now sandpaper against her skin. The tiny spiders spread across the corners of the ceiling looked at her in pity now, and dissolved into the cracks in the wall.

Two more years. I can do two more years.

“Okay, mother,” she said, swallowing it down.

And then her alarm went off.

***

“Damn it.”

Wanda struck her pencil hard against the paper, crossing out her previous attempt at a solution. The nib of the graphite split under the force, and she groaned. This was the third time now that she’d completely messed up the math on what should have been a very basic calculation.

She leaned back in her chair, and tiredly rubbed her eyes until shapes began to form behind them. In truth, the entire morning had been a wash. She’d missed the Columbia stop twice on her commute, she’d forgotten to bring her laptop to her morning lecture, and then halfway through that lecture her students informed her she was giving the same lecture as last week, verbatim, and, to make matters even better, she’d passed out the answer sheet to next week’s exam instead of a blank handout. So she’d have to write a new exam.

All thanks to that stupid nightmare.

“I need a walk,” she announced. Not that anyone heard it. Unlike the other professors, she didn’t share an office. That was a very important stipulation of her contract. Still, sometimes she had to speak out loud to herself in order to force a thought through the incessant noise of her subconscious.

And it was certainly one of those days.

She abandoned her work and exited into the crowded hallway. Usually she brought her notes with her everywhere, just in case inspiration struck, but she knew it would be for nothing today. A singular problem occupied her mind, and she would be unable to fixate on anything else until she solved it.

And that problem was, from the sound of it, teaching a seminar down the hall.

As Wanda stalked towards the familiar voice, she became more and more certain. The nightmare had to belong to Bella. Wanda saw no other clear candidate. Now that she was mature in her powers, she only ever picked up the fears of people she was in close proximity to. Not just physically, but… emotionally. It was why she tried to keep such a hard wall between herself and the world. She saw no need in inheriting the trauma of strangers.

And yet you invited a stranger to live with you.

It was completely inexplicable. She wouldn’t even invite Agatha over for lunch.

She tried not to dwell on it as she arrived at the door to Lecture Hall 304A.

Thankfully, it was already slightly ajar, so she felt less like a stalker.

You’re not a stalker. She invited you to watch.

She sighed, thinking who am I even justifying myself to?

And then tugged the door handle softly, so it didn’t creak.

To her shock, Wanda almost immediately ran into the back of a student as she stepped inside. 304A was one of the department’s larger lecture halls, built in tiers that wrapped around the front like an amphitheater, and seated around a hundred and fifty when full. Today, though, it felt closer to two hundred. Students sat cross-legged by the stage, while others pressed against the back walls, trying to catch a glimpse.

A glimpse of… Wanda’s new roommate.

Wanda grimaced. It was embarrassing how well she understood the mopey, awestruck looks the male students wore as Bella sorted through her lecture notes, her nose scrunching endearingly as she paged through a stick stack of papers.

The blonde had changed out of her work uniform and into a white, deep V-cut blouse that tucked neatly into perfectly ironed blue jeans. The shirt wasn’t revealing enough to be unprofessional, but it was certainly, um…

Jesus Christ. I’m acting like I just got off the Mayflower.

“Professor Maximoff?”

Wanda’s head snapped like an owl when someone tapped her on the shoulder. Her cheeks burned; she felt like she’d been caught in the act, although she wasn’t sure what act that would be, unless going through an internal crisis about a human woman’s cleavage had recently been outlawed in the constitution.

“Yes? What?” she hissed through her teeth. She vaguely recognized the face of the girl who was talking to her. Probably one of her new batch of graduate students. Couldn’t be a notable one, though. She committed the smart ones to memory.

When she spoke, even at the low volume, a few more heads flipped to face her. She had pressed herself as far into the wall as possible to avoid people noticing her, but being the single redheaded professor on the staff was not helpful in staying low profile.

“I didn’t realize you were working with Dr. Dragomir,” the girl said.

Wanda’s mouth opened, then closed. “I’m not. I’m just… dropping in.”

“Oh.” The girl looked very unenthused by this answer. “That’s too bad. I was hoping your course might engage with some of her research. I wanted to take her lecture, but it filled up within minutes of the registration going online. It’s insane. So I’m only getting to audit it. And some days when I get here it’s so busy I don’t even have some place to stand.”

Wanda wanted to feel offended at how this student had very clearly insinuated that she’d rather have taken Bella’s lecture over her own, but her own curiosity overshadowed it. Classes taught by post-docs weren’t usually very tantalizing for masters students. They typically preferred getting to work with established professors: the kind of people who could write recommendations that would get them competitive positions down the line. Post docs didn’t have that same sort of sway in the world of academia.

Which meant Bella’s research had to be actually interesting.

It also meant they clearly were not paying her enough.

“If the room’s this full, why don’t you just watch her lectures online?” Wanda said with a shrug, leaning back into the wall. All lectures were recorded for online viewing nowadays. She let her students choose if they wanted to come in person or not.

“Oh. That’s because Dr. Dragomir doesn’t allow herself to be recorded.”

That got Wanda’s head to turn again. “What?” She let out a disbelieving laugh. “She doesn’t have her lectures recorded? Why?”

The girl shrugged. “She’s a little bit strange about cameras in general. She has everyone turn in their phones at the beginning of class.”

Wanda’s eyebrows lifted comically.

“She takes people’s phones away and her class is still packed? She must be a witch.”

“Eh hem.”

Unlike her good friend Agatha, Wanda prided herself on being aware of her speaking volume. But apparently nothing about Wanda was operating correctly this morning. Her eyes anxiously slid towards the stage, and found exactly what she feared—Bella Dragomir had stopped sorting her notes.

One arm balancing casually on the podium, her finger tapping her maroon-painted lips, she was staring straight at Wanda. As was every other head in the audience.

“Huh. It seems we have a guest.”

Dear god smite me now.

Wanda gave her, and every watching student, a tight-slipped smile. “I apologize for my volume. I was just discussing something with a student. Dr. Dragomir, please.”

She gave her a hand gesture that she hoped signified Get on with it and stop attracting attention to the fact that I can’t stop thinking about you to the point where I’m now forgoing my life’s work to stand in the corner of a packed lecture hall and watch you talk about mold.

It was a silly infatuation, that was all it was. Wanda had endured a handful of them in a thousand years. It simply wasn’t everyday she ran into a human that was both objectively, nauseatingly beautiful and also mildly (perhaps even moderately) intelligent. She’d get over it in a few days and kick the girl out of her house. Politely, of course. With enough money in Bella’s bank account to make up for any hurt feelings.

That’s all she was. A blip. It would peter out into nothing soon enough.

“Why don’t you come up to the podium, Professor,” said the blip, throwing Wanda out of her internal coping session. “It’s not everyday I have a living legend in my class.”

Oh, for the love of God.

“No, no. I’m good,” Wanda said. “This is your class. The living legend will leave.”

Bella crossed her arms across her chest and laughed.

“Nonsense. Do we want Professor Maximoff to leave?”

It was a testament to Bella’s future as a cult leader that the entire class immediately said No we don’t in perfectly synchronous chorus.

To make matters worse, Bella looked her straight in the eye and mouthed Come here, bending her finger beckoningly. There would be no escaping.

Resigning herself to her fate, Wanda rolled her eyes and tried to make it look like it was completely her choice when she jogged down the amphitheater steps towards the stage. Bella offered her a hand to help her up, and even though Wanda didn’t need the help, she took it anyway, ignoring the jolt of electricity she felt in the brief moment of contact.

“Thanks,” Wanda muttered.

Bella hummed, then leaned her back against the podium so that she was just facing Wanda, out of view of the students. And since there were no cameras recording the session, Wanda knew it really was just them when the blonde gave her a small, almost sheepish smile and said, quietly, “Hi.”

Wanda felt her throat swell up.

“Hi,” she whispered back.

“You actually came to watch me talk.”

“I… did. I mean, you invited me.”

Bella twirled the pencil she was holding between her fingers. It almost looked like an anxious tick. But why would Bella be nervous? She was clearly in her element.

“I’m a bit nervous about starting now,” Bella confessed. “My lecture, I mean.”

Wanda blinked, completely thrown off. She looked back at the students; they were talking a bit amongst themselves, but the great majority of the class was still just sitting, staring, waiting. It was like they were under some kind of spell.

“You have no reason to be nervous,” Wanda said. “You’ve booked a full house to talk about…” Wanda’s eyes flitted to the screen above them. “Fungal Enzymes That Interrupt Cellular Breakdown. Do you know how rare this level of attendance is?”

“I’m not nervous about the students,” Bella said. “I’m nervous because of you.”

Wanda froze. She felt her lungs constrict slightly. Bella said it so off-hand.

But then again, it made sense. Wanda was a Distinguished Professor, high-ranking in the department faculty. If she wanted, she could pull ropes to get Bella a position after her post- doc period ended. How naive she’d been to forget that. She’d been so fixated on how she saw Bella, she’d forgotten how Bella probably saw her.

“Oh. Well,” Wanda said, dusting off her sleeves just to give her anxious hands something to do. “That’s even sillier, then. I’m not worth getting nervous about. I promise whatever you say today will not affect my later decision in hiring you to join the permanent teaching staff.” A beat passed where Wanda just stared at her hands, writhing in guilt. Then Bella did something which was becoming all too common between them—and laughed in her face.

“Wanda, I don’t care about your recommendation.”

Wanda’s head snapped up.

“What?” she said. “You don’t?”

“No,” Bella said, then she took a step closer, so Wanda’s world narrowed just to the two of them, and the faint glow of the projector behind Bella’s head, giving her a false halo. “But I do want to work with you. In your lab. You possess a frankly mindboggling amount of funding, way too much for a single researcher to work with in a human lifetime, and I want some of those funds for my own projects. So, yes, I need you to sit there in the front row, and I need you to watch me very intently, and then after that, I need you to leave this lecture just as mesmerized as everyone else is, so you have no choice but to let me steal a bit of your funding. Okay?”

Wanda knew somewhere in the back of her head that there were two hundred adolescent faces staring at them right now. She knew that logically, but no part of her could register it anymore. Not when Bella’s eyes, yellower now, like two blinding lightbulbs, were looking unmistakably at her lips, telling Wanda to watch her very intently.

If Wanda didn’t know better, she would have thought this girl was Suggesting her.

And maybe she was. Because in that moment, Wanda would have given her a lot more than just funding. She would have given her a yacht on the moon.

"People wouldn't usually be so upfront," Wanda said, helpless to say anything else.

"And I'm very familiar with how little you think of most people," Bella said, the smallest smirk forming on her face.

Wanda blinked. She couldn't find something more attractive if she tried.

But she did still have some dignity she intended to preserve.

“We’ll see,” she replied quietly, reaching up to Bella’s blouse, and adjusting the V slightly, under the guise of professionalism. The other woman’s lips parted in surprise. “We can talk about it at my house. After the workday. Over some coffee.”

Bella's face fell. "Wanda, I can't possibly stay another night. I want you to respect my work, not view me as a charity cas—"

But before Bella could finish her sentence, someone in the audience cleared their throat.

“Dr. Dragomir. Professor Maximoff. I apologize for interrupting, but we’re cutting into class time.”

Containing the rabid beating in her chest, Wanda released Bella’s blouse, and turned to the student.

the one where agatha shoplifts & gets in a screaming match with a girl in the dressing room

If Agatha wasn’t a thousand year old vampire, and instead just your normal, run-of-the-mill capitalist sadist, she’d probably run a department store.

“I just don’t think you should legally be able to sell hand soap for more than five dollars,” Agatha muttered into the nook of her neck, which was awkwardly holding her iPhone. Her hands were otherwise preoccupied. “If you get above that price point, I better be washing myself with secretions from an extinct species of whale.”

She finished scratching the tester label off the soap and slipped it in her handbag.

“Ma’am.”

Shit.

Someone cleared their throat directly behind her; close enough that they definitely saw that.

Agatha spun to face this offending onlooker, who turned out to be a twenty-something girl with eye-liner heavy waterlines and a deep-set frown. Agatha smiled; maybe she wouldn’t even need to Suggest this one. She looked one bad day away from quitting her job anyway.

“You didn’t see anything,” Agatha said with a wink. “Can you show me where the changing rooms are? I want to try on a few things.”

The girl studied her for a moment. Agatha hoped she wouldn’t do something stupid like call the police, or worse, the store manager. Agatha could Suggest her, but using her powers tired her out, and she was already exhausted. Infants will do that to you. Soul-sucking little cretins.

After a beat, the girl finally spoke up. “If you’re going to shoplift something, I’d pick a better soap.”

Agatha snorted, instantly relieved.

“I knew you were my kind of people. Wanda, hold on a second.” She took the soap out from her handbag and showed it to her new accomplice. “I only chose this one because it says baby safe and fragrance free but is baby safe even a federally-regulated term? “Pretty low bar for motherhood,” the twenty-something said, taking the soap and giving it a bored once-over. “Yeah, your son should probably be fine. I think. I don’t know. I don’t have kids. Or want them.”

They were beautiful. Agatha kissed them every morning.

Evanora Harkness’s daughter.

She’d known Wanda for over eight hundred years, and not once had she seen her so much as hold pinkies with another person. There had been Richard, of course, the thrall-husband, but he had always been a cover for her. No one questions the wife’s finances when she has a rich husband. It was always assumed her money came from him.

But as far as real romance, there had been nothing. She knew Wanda slept around occasionally, discreetly. Back during one of Agatha’s homeless stints, she’d stayed over at Wanda’s place for a year, and she’d brought back a girl for dinner and drinks.

The mansion had been large enough that Agatha could listen through the vents, and she’d overheard the most boring conversation of her life—some perfunctory bullshit about the weather before Wanda eventually asked, “So, should we have sex now?”

Thinking back to it, Agatha had to stifle a laugh. Wanda was a very straightforward person. She didn’t do messy. She didn’t even drink from the humans she slept with.

Hell, she didn’t drink from humans at all.

Even with all her money, she only drank from the blood bank. It’s more ethical that way, Wanda would insist. Agatha held the opposite view. Life was too long to be ethical. She wasn’t put here on Earth with her good looks and fantastic personality to live like a monk.

Agatha covered the receiver on the phone and shouted back, “Um, I’m sorry, are you one of those inflatable tube men that get blown around by a gust of wind? The dress is made of satin, it should not have been able to knock you over.”

The woman let out a shrill sound of disbelief. The porcelain feet disappeared, the stall door whisked open, and a moment later Agatha’s own stall door was being yanked ajar.

Backlit by the horrible fluorescent overheads was a skyscraper of a woman. She was a cherry blonde, aggressively well-dressed, fisting three overly stuffed shopping bags with her right hand. Her left was pointing at Agatha with complete derision.

The woman’s slightly yellowed eyes looked her up, down, then settled on, “Bitch.”

Agatha snorted. It was so simple, so singular, it left her momentarily speechless. By the time she was ready to respond, the woman was already turning away from her.

“Let’s go, Daniela,” the woman said, eyes fixed on someone Agatha couldn’t see; likely someone in the dressing room waiting area. “Mother’s going to wonder why we’ve been gone so long. And we haven’t hit our quota for today.”

“Noo. Cassandra, please. We can’t leave the mall yet,” whined another voice. “I’ve never been to a Sephora before. I’ll die if I don’t go.”

“It’s literally just a store that sells makeup. Bucharest has those, too.”

Bucharest?

The apparently Romanian skyscraper—Cassandra?—didn’t give Agatha another glance before stalking off toward the cash register. Another woman around her same height saw her flash by and followed behind her like a lost dog.

Agatha stared after them for a long moment, feeling a foreign sense of familiarity. Like Deja Vu, almost. But she couldn’t place it.

“Agatha?” Wanda shouted loudly. “Have you stopped harassing the public yet?”

Agatha laughed, coming out of her reverie.

“Yes, I’m done, I’m done,” she said, grabbing her things before hopping over to Cassandra’s abandoned stall to pick up her dress. She reached down for it then frowned at the texture; rubbing the fabric in between her fingers, it felt brittle now, like it’d been sucked dry. The threading came apart just with a smudge of her thumb.

“So much for quality materials,” she muttered, letting it droop back onto the floor. She sighed; the dress wasn’t going to help her anyway.

It was just a bandaid.

Agatha lifted her bag back onto her shoulder and walked out. “So, you were just telling me how this girl is too smart for you to have sex with her?”

“That is not what I said.”

Agatha grinned. “Kind of what you said, though.”

“It’s not that. I just… I’ve decided to hire her into my lab. And I’m not going to have sex with one of my employees. It’s a… ethical dilemma. Not to mention it’ll distract from the work."

Agatha stopped in her tracks.

Had she heard that correctly?

“Wanda,” she paused. “You’ve never invited anyone into your lab. Any of your labs. You've never let anyone even look at your research.”

The other line went silent. Agatha stood there and waited; shopping carts squeaked by. Women dragged their wailing children into the home goods aisle. After a beat, there was finally that echo again: shoes clopping against hardwood.

“Well,” Wanda said. “No one had ever been impressive enough for me to try.”

The one where shit starts to get SAUCY

It was a perfectly normal morning… at first.

The grandfather clock in Wanda’s living room struck six as she padded in, her slippers dragging across the hardwood. Rays of sunlight were rolling through the floor-to-ceiling glass, and she basked in the slight burn of it on her skin as she fell onto the couch.

Sun used to give her lava-red hives, now it just felt tingly. Such were the perks of living a thousand years; it was plenty of time to get over an allergy.

A stack of untouched and ungraded exams was waiting for her on the coffee table. She’d successfully put them off for a couple days now, but then the emails had started piling up, and there was hardly anything more annoying than a desperate student. The first email was always cordial, then the second was like a diary entry. Sick grandparent. Credit card debt. A hundred reasons why Wanda needed to give them a chance to redo an exam that hadn’t even been graded yet.

Groaning, she snatched them from the table, and lowered her reading glasses over her eyes. She began to skim the answers, a frown carving deeper into her features as she read each subsequent test question.

Wanda always went into every semester with an open mind that she might find a student fit to join her lab, but going by these exams, it seemed that America’s literacy crisis had finally breached the walls of her classroom.

None of these graduate students would be touching her research with a ten foot pole.

Speaking of a ten foot pole— “You finally giving in?”

Wanda’s eyes flicked up, and her cheeks burned.

Ah, yes. There went her naive sense of normalcy.

She’d nearly forgotten about her barista-turned-colleague-turned roommate.

Her unnaturally tall and grossly beautiful roommate, who was wearing a long New York Yankees shirt, Lululemon leggings, and holding two mugs of steaming coffee.

“Yes, unfortunately,” Wanda said, feeling suddenly uncomfortable in her own home. “I thought you’d be at work already.”

It had been two weeks since Wanda made the wonderful decision to invite her somewhat- homeless coworker to live with her, and she’d mostly managed to avoid the consequences so far.

They had vastly different seminar schedules, and Bella liked to be out the door early so she could change at the office, so the two avoided each-other very well. Until today, apparently.

“I brought some of my clothes back here, so I have some more time today,” Bella said with a shrug, then gestured to the couch. “Mind if I join you?”

Wanda shifted her gaze to the left, at the side of the couch that never saw any use. Almost all of Wanda’s furniture was antique, designed during a time when people were smaller and houses less spacious, so it was quite cozy for two people.

“The armchair is very comfortable,” Wanda offered, pointing across the table. A beautiful mahogany leather chair stood in complete disuse. “These couch cushions sink like the Titanic when two people are on it. I don’t want to crowd you.”

Bella laughed. This information was meant to deter her. It failed.

“Let me take a look at those exams,” Bella said instead, setting the coffee on the table and then falling right next to Wanda.

Predictably, the cushions sank, and Bella was practically pressed flush to Wanda’s side. Wanda’s chest tightened at the contact; Bella didn’t seem to care. She just reached over Wanda’s arm and plucked the stapled sheet of papers.

Bella’s eyes studied the text, and Wanda found herself, in absence of something else to do, studying Bella.

Unsurprisingly, she was a fascinating subject. Despite the fact that she’d clearly just woken up, all her jewelry was already in: small silver studs on her earlobes, two rings on each finger, a half-moon necklace dangling over defined collarbones. Her makeup was already dutifully applied, too: foundation, mascara, that damn lipstick.

She clearly takes her appearance very seriously. I don’t think I’ve ever seen her barefaced.

Wanda thought back to that nightmare. The gowns in the closet. Those were ballgowns. Very few families in America would own those in this day and age. Was she from the south? Had she been one of those rich debutante girls?

Stylistically, it made sense. She could see Bella twirling around in a white dress with some cookie cutter future-truck-driver by her side.

But everything she’d alluded to about her home life pointed towards a childhood on the poverty line. Lower middle class at best. Maybe they used to be rich, then had a fall from grace?

Wanda’s skin was starting to itch.

She’d been polite long enough, she decided.

Bella turned the page, and let out a shock of laughter. “Wow. These exams are catastrophically bad.”

“I know,” Wanda said, then licked her lips. “Question. Are you American?”

Bella’s eyebrows rose, and the exam packet fell into her lap. Wanda had asked Bella where she was from before, back at the Nightingale, but she’d dutifully avoided the question.

It seemed she was intent on doing the same now, as she kept her eyes fixed ahead and reached for one of the mugs of coffee. She offered it to Wanda with a nudge.

“Come on,” Bella said. “Don’t let it get cold.”

“Bella.”

Bella’s coy smile dropped.

“What?” she said, sounding annoyed.

“Are you here illegally? Is that why you keep evading the question? Because I hope you know I am the last person on Earth to care about that kind of thing.”

Wanda would have happily forged her some papers if she just asked, but she wasn’t going to volunteer that kind of service out of nowhere.

Bella went silent. Her hand absentmindedly clutched the half-moon pendant.

“I’m not here illegally,” she said after a beat, propping her elbow up on the back of the couch and looking carefully at Wanda. “I’d rather discuss something else.”

“Why?”

“It’s a touchy subject.”

“I see,” Wanda said, bringing her leg up on the couch so she could turn her body completely towards Bella when she asked, “Are you from Romania?”

Bella laughed in shock. “God, you are persistent.”

Wanda shrugged. She’d been told as much before. “I’m just doing basic reconnaissance. Letting you live with me is one thing. Letting a stranger into my lab, around my research, is another thing altogether.”

And okay, that was kind of true. Mostly Wanda was nosy, and Bella was the definition of a closed book, so naturally she felt the urge to tear her pages open.

But also, her research was her world. Even though she didn’t plan to have Bella jump into it straight away, she did need to feel comfortable that she wasn’t a foreign spy intent on stealing it. That would be annoying.

Wanda was torn abruptly from her thoughts when Bella’s hands coiled tightly around her arms. Her mouth was slightly agape, her eyes wide.

“Wait, are you shitting me? You’re actually letting me join your lab?”

Oh, damn it.

So, this phenomenon happened to Wanda embarrassingly often where she would tell Agatha something, then assume she’d told everybody else that thing, too.

“Oh. I suppose… Well, yes. Your lecture was… slightly impressive, and you’ve seen the kind of graduate students I’m working with…”

Bella surged forward, wrapping Wanda up in a bone-cracking hug.

“Oh my god. Thank you, thank you, thank you.”

Bella’s breath landed hot on Wanda’s ear. Encouraged by the natural dip in the couch, the blonde had completely engulfed her: her knees nudging into Wanda’s stomach, her hand tight around the back of Wanda’s neck, not letting go.

“You have no idea what this means to me,” Bella confessed quietly.

It sounded uncharacteristically earnest.

In the silence, Bella’s nails scratched along the base of Wanda’s neck. It was incredibly intimate, and it also, very obviously, had to have been a mistake; Bella was emotionally overwhelmed, not thinking about her actions, not taking account of her hands—but it was enough of a shock to get Wanda to speak.

“Exactly. I don’t know,” Wanda replied, pulling back. “That’s what makes me so hesitant about involving you.”

Bella’s fingers froze against Wanda’s neck. She took in a breath, and then peeled herself off, extricating her limbs.

Wanda was eternally grateful for it. Any longer enduring that kind of proximity and she would have… God…

“Well, if working together really necessitates total honesty…” Bella trailed off, pulling her knee up to her chest, then perching her chin on it. “I can tell you about how I got to Columbia, but I might as well start with the bigger potential issue threatening our scientific partnership.”

Wanda stared at her, befuddled.

“Huh? What bigger issue?”

Bella looked shyly down at her socks, as if she was counting the stripes on them. After a long moment, she trailed her eyes back up to Wanda’s face.

“Well, for one,” Bella sighed. “I find you really, really attractive.”

Bella’s hand fell over Wanda’s knee, giving it a light squeeze, like a zookeeper trying to reason with a frightened animal.

“Hey, are you okay?” she prodded.

She had the audacity to look worried. This woman.

“Of course I’m okay. I’m an adult,” Wanda said, but it came out strangled.

To give herself a semblance of control, she backed up until there was nowhere else on the couch to go, which was only about two inches.

How dare it be so miserably small? She would be replacing it with one of those supersized IKEA monstrosities as soon as this conversation was over.

“An adult who is crawling backwards away from me.”

“I’m not…” Wanda said, then, realizing she, of course, was, she took in a humiliated breath, and pinched her nose. “Are you fucking with me?”

“What? No. I wouldn’t lie about finding someone attractive,” she said, then arched an eyebrow. “Would you lie about something like that?”

Wanda blinked. How the hell is she turning this around on me?
