But then, if that were true, why was she lying now? Her mother was as unpredictable as the weather in April. She was a cunning woman, but she was also an overconfident one, and her pride was her sore spot.

A sore spot that apparently hadn’t faded in their time apart.

As Wanda bent down to clear her throat, Alcina’s eyes met hers. “You lied to us,” she mouthed. She didn’t look angry, which was even scarier than the alternative. “You said she’d be in Los Angeles.”

Bella visibly winced. It would have been a fine lie, if not for Charlie Smith.

“I didn’t…” Bella began, but then Wanda was already standing back up, having successfully gotten her drink down her esophagus.

“God,” Wanda laughed. “I’m so happy you three could make it in Charlie’s stead. You are so much more fun than that idiot drunkard.”

Under the table, Wanda found Bella’s hand. Bella startled when she intertwined their fingers and gripped her tightly.

Confused, she looked at Wanda’s eyes. Was she drunk? She didn’t look drunk.

“We are so pleased we could come,” Alcina said. “We’ve heard a lot about you overseas. About your great collection.” She made a complimentary gesture towards the paintings on the wall. “Here I thought it was all rumor. Well, some of it must be, at least. I would assume we would have spotted him tonight, otherwise.”

Wanda’s smile tightened, as did her grip on Bella’s hand.

“Him?” Wanda said breathily. “I’m not sure I know who you mean.”

Bella felt the air grow thinner. She had no idea who they could possibly be talking about. It wasn’t like Wanda was hiding a secret husband.

“Your son, of course.”

Alcina lifted her glass to her lips. Wanda’s stare went blank.

Bella had spent enough time around Wanda to be able to read her pretty well by now. She had a very expressive face. If she had a thought, she wore it.

She couldn’t read Wanda’s face at all then.

“Hypothetical son,” Alcina corrected. “Seems I was lied to.”

Wanda let go of Bella’s hand. She used it to dig into her pocket and seamlessly take out her phone. She dialed 1, then slid it back in her pants.

Bella’s heart felt like a racehorse in her chest. What was she doing?

“Seems you were,” Wanda said. She smiled then, at Cassandra and Daniela. It looked strangely genuine. “But look at your daughters. They’re beautiful.”

“Er, yes. They are.”

Alcina’s guise briefly faltered. Bella could not for the life of her grasp what was happening; that was a clear attempt from her mother to unsettle Wanda. She knew that much. And for a moment, it seemed to work—until it didn’t.

“Are you two married?” Wanda asked her sisters, sipping the last of her drink. She flagged over a waiter for another.

When she and Bella were children, Cassandra would practice smiling in the mirror. She flashed that exact one now as she brought up her hand. “No ring yet.”

Daniela did the same, but with a frown. “I thought I found the one last year, but he left me for another vampire he met on Instagram. Some whore from Miami.”

Wanda laughed again, and it sounded completely earnest. She looked at Bella then, eyes crinkled, as if she wanted to share the moment with her. As if this was something special between them. Bella didn’t understand. She wanted to.

“His loss,” Wanda said, turning away as the waiters served them more drinks. “But that’s alright. There’s more to life than that. Any hobbies?”

Alcina looked like she was about to interrupt, but Daniela beat her to it, nodding eagerly. She got out her phone—the newest iPhone, with a Hello Kitty case—and faced it to Wanda as she scrolled through her photo album. It was full of pictures of… credit cards. Some of them were cut up with scissors.

“Credit card churning,” Daniela said. “You collect all the sign-up bonuses, spend the money, then cancel the card. It’s so fun. Ca–Vivianna taught me how.”

Bella was impressed she remembered to use the fake name at all.

“A terrible decision,” Cassandra interjected. When Wanda looked at her expectantly, Cassandra huffed, as if she was above the question. “I don’t do hobbies.”

Wanda smiled sadly at her. “You don’t do anything for fun?”

Cassandra’s eyes darted to Bella then, ever briefly, before turning back to Wanda. Bella hated that she could read her mind, even so many years later. They were both thinking of what they used to do for fun, back when fun was an option.

They’d climb to the highest point in their village—a crumbling, abandoned watchtower—and perch like birds at the top. Then they’d grip each other's hands, let their wings burst out of their backs, and they’d sail down until they tumbled into the weeds, giggling as they spit dirt out of their mouths.

“I do my nails sometimes,” Cassandra murmured, flashing them to Wanda. They were a beautiful blue, painted over with acrylic ocean waves.

Wanda held her hand, inspecting the art for a long moment.

“They’re really nice. You have a talent.”

Cassandra blushed—a rare sight—and jerked her hand back. Alcina glared at her for her impoliteness, and she reddened some more.

“Thank you,” Cassandra replied, like a reprimanded child.

“You’re very welcome.” Wanda took a final sip of her drink, emptying it. Then she sighed, and fixed her gaze back at Alcina. “You raised three great daughters.”

The buzz of voices and clinking glasses and waiters peeling around in circles all fell suddenly and devastatingly quiet on Bella’s ears. Does she..? No…

“Two, you mean,” Alcina said slowly, eyes moving between Wanda and Bella.

“I meant three.” Wanda set down her glass, then glanced toward Bella. Bella gaped. Panicked. This entire time she thought Wanda had been clueless.

“Wanda…” Bella began. It felt as if her throat was swelling. Would Wanda assume she was in on the lie with them? No, no, no. Her breath came quickly. “I didn’t…”

Wanda squeezed her hand and gave her a small, reassuring smile. At the same time, there was a heavy knock on the front door. Bruce cleared his throat, straightened his suit—anticipating another gaggle of guests.

“So, there are a few options here,” Wanda said calmly, absentmindedly playing with the tips of her dragon-red hair. “You all can stay for dinner, and we can have a great evening. I’ll break out my favorite red from 17th century Italia, and over a few glasses you can tell me why you decided to burn down my house. After that, maybe we play cards. A boardgame. Charades. We can have a merry, merry, time. Then you can leave, and we can stay in touch, send letters, that sort of thing.”

Wanda lifted her right hand, and dark, wispy smoke curled around her fingers. Bella was taken aback; she didn’t know Wanda had any sort of tangible power. Her mother seemed to share her surprise—although only in a way that Bella could spot, having learned to master Alcina’s micro-expressions—her teeth tightened slightly.

“Alternatively,” Wanda said, eyes melting to a violent crimson. “You can continue to silently torment your daughter from across the table. Listen, I’m usually the last person to intervene in another family’s personal issues, but unfortunately, I really like Bella.” Wanda’s eyes flitted almost shyly over to her for a split-second, and a warmth poured through Bella’s clenched chest. “So, if it will help her to stop vibrating with terror next to me, I will have no problem handing you off to my friends, who—look at that—just arrived. There they are.”

Two figures strode into the ballroom, neither dressed for the occasion. Agatha was wearing a hoodie and sweatpants, and Rio’s hair was still wet from the shower.

They waved. And Alcina laughed.

“Oh my,” she said, absolutely tickled. “Is that…”

“The Harkness daughter,” Cassandra hissed, face pale. “And Lady Death.”

“Aren’t they known for, like, killing everyone?” Daniela whispered innocently.

“Don’t be rude, Daniela,” Alcina said, and the use of the name itself was a small concession towards Wanda. “But yes. What a great party this is. Such exciting guests. We so rarely meet real celebrities back in Romania. Right girls?”

Daniela nodded easily. Cassandra seemed too stunned to do anything.

Clipping her purse shut, Alcina shot Wanda an apologetic smile. “Which makes it too bad that we have to leave early. We told the driver we’d only stay for drinks. Cassandra, Daniela, tell him to get the car started will you?”

The sisters nodded obediently. Cassandra gave Bella one more look—a frenzied, almost scared glance—before dragging Daniela towards the foyer. They bumped shoulders with Aster on their way out, and Cassandra turned even whiter.

Alcina and Wanda studied each other in silence, both smiling tightly. Bella’s entire body was frozen in fear. All these superficial niceties would only last as far as Alcina wanted to take them. But she had sent her daughters away, so that had to mean something—perhaps a genuine retreat.

“My dear Bella.” Alcina’s gaze turned to her, and Bella could see the act drop in real time. She understood at once why she’d sent Cass and Daniela to the car. “You have no idea what I’ve put up with in your absence. Your absolutely useless sisters.” Alcina let out a huff. “It doesn’t surprise me in the least that you’ve acquired such a prestigious friend. You’ve always had that magic touch.”

Bella’s stomach sank as far down as a shipwreck. Praise, praise, praise. That was always her mother’s strategy. Throw her sisters under the bus, then drown Bella in reasons why she was better than them.

“They aren’t useless, mother,” she said quietly.

Alcina laughed, waved her hand.

“You don’t need to defend them. They can’t hear you. Now, come on, go join them by the vehicle. Ms. Maximoff and I have to discuss a few more things, then…”

“Join them?” Wanda interjected with a laugh. “She’s not leaving with you.”

Bella shot Wanda a terrified look. What was she doing? Of course Bella didn’t want to get in the car with them; no, it was the last thing she wanted; but she knew she could endure whatever Alcina had planned for her if it meant this was over.

And that Wanda was safe.

“Yes, she is,” Alcina said, eyebrows furrowing—she looked absolutely confused at the idea of Bella not following an order. “Bella, go.”

“No,” Wanda said, gripping Bella’s wrist. “She’s staying right here.”

Alcina’s eyes slid between them.

“Hm. Is that so,” she said, like she was appraising a piece of furniture. “Well, perhaps I can loan her to you for a bit longer. In fact, that can serve as a basis for our negotiation. You get my gold star for the week, and you lend me yours.”

Alcina pointed over Wanda’s shoulder towards Rebecca, who was watching the whole affair from a distance with thinly veiled concern.

Wanda balked. “My assistant? She is not up for sale.”

“Come on, darling. Everything is, for the right price. It will only be a week. I just need someone who can get our affairs in order before we travel back home.”

She’s lying, Bella wanted to shout, but it stayed clenched between her teeth. She didn’t know what her mother wanted Rebecca for, but it probably had to do with the contract. And anything that got her closer to an enthralling device was a horrible idea. So Bella shook her head vigorously and finally stopped being a wet towel.

“Anything you need arranged, I can arrange it, Mother,” she said, letting go of Wanda’s hand even as she ached to hold it. “You don’t need to poach Wanda’s employees. I can book plane tickets and hotels just as well as any human.”

Alcina shot her that irritated smile that said don’t interrupt when Mommy’s working. It was the same one that Bella received when humans would come to visit their shop back in Dacia, and eager, friendly little Bella would try and strike up a conversation with the foreign customers.

Her mother didn’t like her fraternizing too much with the food.

“I’m sorry, but no,” Wanda said, stepping away from the table. “No Rebecca, no Bella. No gift bags. This is not a birthday party.”

“Wanda—” Bella began, but Wanda cut her off, rounding the edge of the table and extending her hand to Alcina.

“It was a pleasure to meet you. Don’t get lost on your way to the door.”

Alcina let out a startled laugh. For the first time, she could not seem to find a way to deflect the conversation back in her favor. Wanda had offered her a brick wall.

Alcina turned with knives in her eyes towards Bella. Bella could see the implicit question in her eyes; it was a guillotine of a question. Who are you loyal to?

It made Bella’s entire body tremble. Her stomach clenched horribly.

Her wings, pressed so deeply into her back that she sometimes forgot they were there, started to itch.

For Bella, this was the worst possible outcome. Worse even than a fight, a deadly one; at least a battle left room for ambiguity. Bella could pretend to be hurt, she could make herself bleed, she could flee and hide in the woods.

This, however, was a binary choice. Staying with Wanda would invite a series of consequences she had spent her entire life living in fear of.

Bella’s body sagged with that weight. The new world had made her feel so very brave and reinvented, this bold new version of herself, untouchable, and yet the illusion of that freedom crumpled under one irritated look from her mother.

Two thousand years old, and she was still the same child hiding in the closet, counting how many days had passed since her mother had locked the door.

She turned to Wanda, and reached out for her hand, squeezing it. “Don’t worry, I won’t miss a day at the lab. I’ll text you once I see them off to the airport, okay?”

Wanda blinked in disbelief. In that small way, Wanda was similar to Alcina—she was thoroughly unused to not getting what she wanted.

It’s not like she actually wants you. She’s just being a good friend.

Wanda gripped her hand, not letting go. “Bella, no.”

“Just have a nice rest of your party. Rebecca put so much effort in.”

Wanda’s eyes were going red again. “Stop it.”

“It’s really not a big deal. It’s fine. I’m fine.”

“But you’re not,” Wanda’s voice broke quietly. “You’re so scared. I can’t take it.”

The devastated expression on Wanda’s face—it felt like someone had buried their hand in Bella’s chest and squeezed her heart with their bare hands. There was so much emotion in her voice; she’d never heard it so stricken.

Bella knew that Wanda’s powers allowed her to sense fear more palpably than other people, but she was usually so calm, so collected—it was hard to imagine that it actually affected her. But she could see it now. Wanda was trembling.

All because of me, Bella realized in dismay. I caused this. She's absorbing all my fear.

A pit the size of a boulder lodged itself in Bella’s throat. She opened up her mouth to say something, anything, to assuage her panic, but then something dripped from the ceiling onto the table. It looked like black sludge.

“Fuck,” Wanda breathed, eyes fixating on the stain. “No, no, no. Get it together.”

Sludge began to dribble out of the walls. Slow at first—eeking out from behind paintings, pushing through the wrinkles in the curtains—until suddenly, it was as if a pipe had burst. A tsunami of black swallowed the room; Alcina shrieked, and her wings burst from her back, so she could fly out of the way of the waves.

Wanda grabbed Bella, pressing her to her chest. “Close your eyes,” she insisted. “Please. I promise it’ll be over soon. It’s not real.”

The entire room had descended into a frenzy. Guests were swimming and running and spilling out like bile into the foyer. The sludge lapped hard at Bella's back, dousing her clothes. It was freezing; as cold as an arctic pool.

“Like hell, it’s not real,” Bella hissed, the cold burning her like she’d just laid her entire body on a hotplate. She carefully pried herself from Wanda’s chest, and held her panicked face delicately. “Wanda, what’s happening?”

Bella saw the reflection of a strange, blurry image in Wanda's eyes when she confessed, “I’m having a nightmare.”

Chapter 25

Chapter Notes

In case you want a visual companion to this piece, I made an edit of bella and wanda

💀 because I’m obsessed https://vm.tiktok.com/ZNRW2BVSP/

It was happening again.

Her heart rabbited in rapid, uneven beats as she turned through the room, watching the pandemonium spread. One guest was climbing up the curtain towards the ceiling, terrified, screaming; another was slamming his fist against the wall, seemingly unaware that it wasn’t a locked door. She didn’t know what visions they were seeing. The nightmare looked different to everyone.

But Wanda saw the monsters.

She’d first met them when she was a child. She’d just returned from a playdate with Tabitha, and she’d snuck under the fence into the wide field where they kept the cattle. That was where she saw Wendy. Wanda’s favorite, the black-spotted heifer with the crooked lip. She was tilted over in the grass. Wanda blamed the neighborhood boys, until she walked over to the cow, and saw its stomach was wide open.

Even though she couldn’t remember her mother’s name anymore, she remembered screaming it as she ran into the house. Her eyes were blurry with tears, and maybe that was a blessing, but also it was a curse, because she never clearly saw the faces of the monsters that she found inside.

Her mind had tried to invent a clear picture of them ever since.

That was the horrible and unproductive thing about the human subconscious. It’s like a pathological detective; it can’t leave a stone unturned. When you endure something terrible, it will search endlessly for little details that might help you go back and fix what happened to you. Wanda wondered for a long time if humans used to have access to time machines, and God had at some point revoked the privilege.

But at least the human imagination was restricted to the measly brain. Wanda’s imagination leapt and shrieked into the present, and now swarming through the air were pale, malnourished bodies, stricken skeletal things, like baby birds without the feathers. They were horrible, stomach-churning creatures. But at this point, Wanda was used to her mind inventing even worse—what actually paralyzed her was the horror on her guests’ faces as the things grabbed them, stopping them from escaping.

The creatures had one of the Kulmars now. The patriarch of the family. A strong businessman with a tight jaw, hundreds of years old, unshakable.

He was crying for his mother.

“Wanda,” Bella’s voice cut through her terror like scissors. “How do I help you?”

Wanda’s breath hitched as she kept her hands tightly wound around Bella’s back, fingernails digging protectively into the fabric of her dress.

In that moment, she realized everyone else could go to hell.

The house—every painting, every teacup set, every rug and chandelier—the demons could ransack every inch. Just not her. Not Bella.

“Just stay still,” Wanda said, voice breaking. “Don’t move. Don’t look at them. They feed on your fear.”

“Them?” Bella asked, voice thin with confusion. “Who are you talking about?”

Wanda was about to explain, when another voice cut in.

“What are you doing waiting around down there?”

Through the wind in Wanda’s ears, Alcina’s voice barely registered. But she could feel Bella tense in her arms. She could feel the bubble of fear around her swell again. No, no, no. Wanda’s entire body pulsed with conviction to keep Bella close. Because if Bella took a single step, the demons would sniff her out. They’d grab her; they’d terrorize her; they’d break her mind and leave her crying.

And Bella would see Wanda for who she really was.

A living host to terrible, terrible things.

“I can’t just leave her like this,” Bella shouted back to her mother.

“Have you lost your mind?” Alcina spat. “Do you not remember why everyone refuses to associate with Wanda Maximoff? Why we are her only guests in decades?”

“No,” Bella's voice wavered. “But it doesn’t matter.”

A tear wet Wanda’s cheek as Bella slowly pulled back, like a ship whose anchor had been violently wrenched away by the sea. The windows blew out, shards of glass flying onto the terrace. The ginormous oaks outside lurched and groaned. The chandelier swung, swung.

Get it together, Wanda tried to tell herself. This would only get worse if she couldn’t put a lid on her emotions. It wasn’t just Bella’s mind at stake; the humans in the house could be killed.

As Wanda tried to concentrate on her breathing, filling her stomach and then emptying it, Alcina suddenly appeared behind Bella, putting a firm, possessive hand on her daughter’s shoulder. The creatures glanced her way, but they didn’t jump her. Their freakish noses— which lacked cartilage, so they were more like two gaping holes—sniffed in her direction, then recoiled, like she was rotten.

Wanda blinked. The nightmare… isn’t affecting her?

Watching their strange reaction, she was reminded of how she found Bella the day of the fire. How the raging flames had reacted similarly, rearing like horses on their back legs, fearful of touching her.

“These powers of hers—she drove her own family into suicidal madness,” Alcina said as her ginormous black wings unfolded on either side of Bella. “She’s a thief of joy. She takes everything pleasant inside of you for herself, and leaves you with a gaping maw staring back at you. I mean, really, her parents had no other choice than to kill each other before they were just hollow sacks.”

“That’s…” Wanda’s lungs felt like they were being filled with nails. Was that really what happened? Had she misunderstood it, all this time? No. No. “That’s not true.” She looked at Bella, desperation filling her entire being. “That’s not true, Bella. She’s lying.”

Bella stared at her, expression unreadable. Despite their closeness, their bodies only inches from each-other, Wanda felt like she was looking at her from across a canyon.

“She really makes you pity her, doesn’t she,” Alcina said quietly, eyes raking up and down Wanda’s face. “And I do, I really do. Pity you. Feel for you. I am no stranger to ostracization myself. But my daughter is too important to me. So you must forgive me for being over- protective. I’m sure you understand.”

Another twist around Wanda’s intestines. This one even worse—because she did understand. She would protect Billy from anything. She would lie cruelly, too, if it meant he would be safe.

Maybe Alcina was right. Maybe Bella would be better off.

No, not maybe—definitely.

Wanda’s eyes darkened, and she let the nightmare pour out of her in tidal waves. Why bother trying to restrain it? There was nothing she could do. She’d had a thousand years to learn and control her demons, and she couldn’t. If she couldn’t keep Bella safe now, how did she hope to do it later? How could she even keep Billy safe? She could do everything to extend his life, and maybe she’d end up killing him in the end. Wouldn’t that be just perfect. Maybe everyone would be better without her.

The roof was shaking. The house was coming down.

She hoped it crushed her.

“Listen to your mom, Bella,” she said, feeling vacant. “She’s right. You should get out of here.”

She turned away from Bella, towards the blank wall. The creatures were waiting for her there, the whole pack of them smiling toothlessly. Their horror was almost comforting to her now. Familiar. Maybe it would be good if she went with them this time. She’d never thought of that before.

Just letting them take her away, back to where they came from.

Maybe that was how she finally escaped this feeling. Maybe that was her ship back home.

She inhaled sharply, then let out a quiet, choked sob, and took a step forward.

But then Bella cleared her throat. “I think you should join Cassandra and Daniela by the car, Mother,” she said, causing Wanda to halt in place. “They must be tired of waiting for you.”

“What?” Alcina snapped.

Bella’s voice gained more conviction. “Wanda is going to need some help clearing up the dinner table. You raised me with better manners than to abandon the host with all the dirty dishes.”

Something warm touched Wanda’s back. The press of a palm against her shoulder blades. It felt like a brief storm of rain during a wildfire, an instant relief. The creatures’ lopsided smiles instantly dissolved from their faces.

Alcina took a moment before responding. During that silence, Wanda didn’t dare move.

“This friendship will not survive the summer. I hope you know that.”

Wanda couldn’t see Bella, but she could feel her fingers press harder on her back.

They felt like an anchor.

“I agree with you, Mother. It won’t,” Bella said, and nothing more.

Wanda’s shoulders dropped, her last breath of hope exhaling with them.

But then Alcina snarled. “God—don’t be so.” She paused, seeming to re-evaluate. “Fine, then, it makes no difference,” she finally decided. “But don’t expect us to wait for you. Fuel is expensive, and I assume Daniela has been sitting there with her feet kicked up on the dashboard, running the air conditioner.”

Alcina waited another minute, leaving room for Bella to change her mind, but she didn’t. She stayed. Even though she didn’t believe their friendship would survive the summer, she was still here, touching her, not backing up an inch. It left Wanda frozen with disbelief; frozen even after she heard Alcina stalk towards the door. Even after it slammed closed, and left only a haunting silence.

“Turn around, Wanda,” Bella whispered.

Wanda looked up, as if for permission. The creatures all shook their heads in one decisive, seething, no.

“No. You should leave,” Wanda replied. “Your mom is right about me. Even if she’s a shitty messenger.”

Bella scoffed, but it wasn’t mean-hearted. “She’s a pathological liar, Wanda. And even if she wasn’t, I know you didn’t kill your parents.”

Wanda saw a flash of their bodies on the floor again. The creatures laughed, as if they were rejoicing with revelry in the memory. She winced.

“You weren’t there,” Wanda snapped. “So how are you to know? I was there, front row and center, and I can barely remember what happened.”

Bella took an unexpected step forward, pushing their bodies flush together; she laced her fingers around Wanda’s stomach. She was as warm and as inviting as a campfire.

“I took an elementary Abnormal Psychology class in freshman year of college, and do you know what I learned?” she asked. “Cold-blooded killers don’t spend their lives consumed with guilt, trying every day to repent for some unabsolvable sin. Nor do they grow up to be really good mothers.”

Mothers. Goosebumps raced down Wanda’s arms. It was almost enough to make her flip around, but she was still too scared; all she could do was tremble in Bella’s arms.

“You have a son,” Bella said in the tenderest, most understanding way. “Not a nephew.”

Wanda wanted to deny it. She wanted to believe she could still keep Billy safe through purposeful omission, but even she knew that veil had long been stripped.

“I… I do,” she confessed, a tear streaking down her cheek. It felt like a release, just to say it. ‘Billy.”

“Billy.” Bella inhaled softly, like that was the most wonderful revelation. “He’s human, isn’t he?”

“Yes.”

“That’s why you want to understand immortality. You don’t want to lose him.”

She’d never quite said that bit out loud, had she? “Yes.”

“Turn around, Wanda. Please. I’m not that scary to look at.”

Wanda let out a shuddering breath. The fleshy, half-human creatures blinked and twitched at her. Bella would certainly be a nicer visual alternative. But there was still something stopping her.

“I can’t,” she whispered, guilt and shame enwrapping her like a winter coat. “If you look me in the eyes, they’ll leech onto you. I can control it under usual circumstances, but not now. It’s still so raw.”

Bella leaned her forehead against Wanda’s shoulder, and, after lingering for a moment, pressed a kiss there. It struck Wanda like a brief shock of lightning.

“Bella, what are you—”

“I just told my mother to fuck off for the first time in my life. First time in two thousand years, mind you,” Bella said, her breath landing hot on Wanda’s exposed skin. “There’s not a demon from hell that can get me off that high. So try me. Look me right in the eyes, and scare me.”

Bella sounded so sure of herself, Wanda almost forgot it was all theatrics. She almost turned around.

“You don’t understand how bad it can get,” Wanda said. “It sends you to a very dark place.”

“The woman you just met once trapped me in an unlit closet for four years. I might actually be uniquely prepared.” Bella pressed another kiss to the nape of Wanda’s neck, like this was some game. “Or, you could just look at me, and if I start to scream, you could just kiss me. That seems to work in the movies.”

Bella didn’t give her an opportunity to come up with another excuse. She grabbed her waist more firmly, then turned her around, forcing her away from the darkness.

And there Bella was, smiling adoringly, with one fang sitting over bottom lip.

And God—fuck—Wanda was—

Wanda was so—

“You have wings,” she observed with a shaky breath, because if she said what she was really thinking, she would have nowhere to hide anymore.

Bella blushed, and gorgeous black feathers folded inward, hiding shyly behind her back.

“They were more useful before helicopters and airplanes. Now I can’t do anything fun without being registered as an unidentified flying object.”

Wanda laughed, actually laughed, and the hissing wind in her ears went down a decibel.

But she was still too scared to look Bella in the eyes.

“I think they’re very nice,” she said instead.

Bella scoffed. “I think you’re very nice. And the wings are ugly, but tolerable.”

“Ugly, but tolerable,” Wanda repeated. “I think you once said something similar about my personality.”

“God, you really always need the last word, don’t you?”

Bella reached for her chin, and Wanda shivered as she raised it up. The movement was so quick and gentle, she couldn’t help the basic instinct to look. Look at Bella’s eyes, which were as red and soft as a field of poppy flowers. Wanda wasn’t sure she’d ever seen such a violent color look so pretty.

But then, almost as soon as she let herself be comforted, she felt it. The jolt of electricity that passed between her and a victim. The nightmare spreading. Bella’s soft eyes darkened, and her smile fell. The creatures flew out from behind her, globbing onto Bella in a giant, fleshy mass.

Wanda cried out. She was about to start tearing them off, prying them off of her with bare hands—even if she knew it was useless—when the mass of monsters pulsed, once, like a heartbeat.

Then disintegrated into dust. Blowing out with the wind, into the oak trees.

And Bella stood there, as unaffected and casual as a gas station girl on her lunch break.

“Huh,” Bella said, blinking several times. Her eyes quickly regained their spritely brightness. “Your powers taste like… like, something I used to eat when I was younger. I can’t really put a name to it. Maybe a custard?”

“I’m sorry, taste?” Wanda sputtered, astonished. “How are—are you okay? How did you do that?”

Bella rolled her eyes in amusement. “Do you usually ask the oven if it’s okay after it burns your food?” When Wanda just looked at her with further bemusement, Bella tucked a hair behind her ear with her gloved hand. “I’m a furnace. My powers disintegrate things. I wasn’t sure it would work on your demons, but it seems I’m an equal opportunity blowtorch.”

“Oh.”

Oh.

Wanda couldn’t believe it.

“So, that makes you…immune,” she said.

“Yeah. Sorry. A bit anti-climactic,” Bella said with a small laugh. “I did think about pretending for a few seconds, so you might try and kiss me Sleeping Beauty-style. But I figured that might backfire.”

Wanda let out a rushed, incredulous laugh. And for the hundredth time since they first met, Wanda felt like maybe she’d dreamt this woman up.

It would have been the first pleasant dream she’d had in a long, long time.

“You smug idiot,” Wanda muttered. “You don’t have to trick me into kissing you.”

And because she really did always need the last word, she leaned forward.

And God; Wanda wrapped her hands around Bella’s slim waist in that gorgeous dress, then trailed them up to her back, where she could trace the rough edges of her wings under the fabric; Bella made a soft whimpering sound that went right to Wanda’s stomach; and Wanda found herself, for the first time in probably eight hundred years, enjoying the simple act of being alive.

***

After an untold amount of wonderful, perfect, seconds, Bella pulled away.

"As pleasant as this is," she said, eyes flitting over Wanda's shoulder. "We should probably address the fact that Rebecca hasn't come to interrupt us."

Wanda blinked. She whipped her head around, to that corner of the room her assistant was always haunting.

It was empty.

"Oh my god."

Chapter 26

Wanda drew slowly apart from Bella, her vision pulsing, her heart still hammering. She'd been so close to saying something. She'd felt the words bubbling up from somewhere deep inside her, clawing their way toward her throat like a ravenous animal.

What had she been about to say?

She glanced at Bella's eyes again, remembering that it was, somehow, by some miracle, a safe thing to do. She'd spent so long being afraid of simple eye contact.

It was almost inconceivable that she could hold Bella's gaze for as long as she wanted, and nothing would come of it.

Nothing would come of it besides Wanda falling deeper in...

Oh.

Oh.

“Wanda.”

Bella's fingers were digging into her collarbones, shaking her slightly. Her touch was warm enough to wrench Wanda from the depths of her mind.

"Did you hear me? I think they took Rebecca."

That was pretty much the only thing that could distract Wanda from the thought that had just assaulted her.

She breathed in, trying to shake it off. She'd just been caught up in the moment, the adrenaline. Thoughts didn’t really describe reality, they were just passing tumbleweeds.

That tumbleweed felt more like an anvil falling from a skyscraper.

Didn’t matter. She could deal with it later. She could deal with everything later. She needed to make sure that Billy was safe. And Rebecca, fuck—

Wanda massaged her temples, trying to get the room to stop spinning. "You think they kidnapped her?"

"That would be the polite word for it. But I don't think they're planning on killing her, at least not until they get what they’re after.” Bella pinched the bridge of her nose in frustration. “Do you know about the mass suggestion contract?"

Wanda’s heart sank to her feet.

"Oh, fuck."

***

Wanda tore through the house. Opening doors, slamming them, not caring when entire load- bearing walls shuddered behind her.

She looked and looked and looked. Not a soul was left aside. Certainly not Rebecca.

The mansion had emptied like a stomach; Wanda’s nightmare had vomited up all of the guests. She ploughed through the space where the front door used to be, and found lingering bits of bile in the form of Agatha, Rio, and Bruce strewn about the garden.

They were all coming down from their fights with their respective demons. Bruce was nursing his head, which he'd probably slammed into the wall while fighting an imaginary bottle of alcohol, or perhaps a rival baseball fan. Agatha’s eyes were bloodshot; she'd probably just had a traumatic conversation with her dead mother. Rio seemed mostly fine, which was kind of annoying, but predictable.

But there was no sign of Rebecca.

Wanda’s heels sunk into the mud as she stalked onto the lawn. There was only one car left in the driveway, and no limousine.

“Did you see Bella’s family leave?” she shouted at Bruce. “Did they take Rebecca?”

It took Bruce several moments to discern what she was talking about. “Take?” he finally said. “I thought you loaned them Ms. Rebecca.”

Wanda groaned.

Oh my God.

“Bruce. I do not loan.” She stabbed her finger to his chest. “Paintings, jewelry, furniture, tupperware, least of all my staff.”

“Oh,” Bruce said, his mouth turning into a perfect o. He added quietly, “Not even me? I might deserve it, Ms. Maximoff. I let them breeze right by.”

Wanda’s eyes softened. He looked completely prepared for her to fire him right then and there. It was unexpectedly hurtful, seeing just how much he expected that reaction. Like she’d just toss him away after all this time.

Worse yet, she knew he was right to feel that way.

Maybe a few months ago, she would have.

She sighed.

“People aren’t dispensable,” she said quietly. Finding a strange, foreign forgiveness inside of her, she continued, “It’s not your fault. You didn't know.”

She saw Agatha, even in her disgruntled state, raise an eyebrow.

“That’s new,” she mused. “When did you start being nice?”

“I’ve always been nice.”

“Not at all,” Agatha snorted. “You’re good. But not nice.”

Wanda found herself unable to argue. She had spent hundreds of years building her persona around making every person in her chain of command completely interchangeable, comparing them to screws and bolts and chains. She’d told Rebecca the day after she hired her: “if I fire you, it’s not personal.” It was just work.

That’s what it always had been. Everything was in service of the work.

Even Wanda was a cog in her own machine. If she’d died, Rebecca knew she was in charge of finding a suitable replacement to finish her research.

And honestly, Rebecca probably would have found an excellent replacement—one far better and more effective than Wanda, if Bella was any indication. That was what made Rebecca so incredible. She drastically improved every plan she touched. She’d viewed Rebecca as a highly effective tool. Now she was realizing that she was more like a replacement for Wanda’s entire prefrontal cortex.

Case in point: standing in the field, frustrated with what to do next, she instantly opened her mouth to yell for Rebecca to solve this situation for her. Then she paused.

“I’m an idiot,” she groaned.

It had been so long that she’d had to use her brain to solve a problem that wasn’t mathematical or scientific in nature. It had been years since she’d paid an electricity bill, or driven a car. Was she really this useless? God.

What would Rebecca do?

She tried to summon the voice of her wonderfully blunt assistant. Rebecca would probably tell her to act quickly. To stop stalling.

“Whose car is that?” Wanda snapped, pointing to the last remaining vehicle in the drive.

“That’s your car, ma'am,” Bruce said.

“Oh.”

Wanda owned so many things, she sometimes felt like one of those “repopulate the Earth” people with fourteen children. Once you get past five, you sort of forget the other ones exist. Same went for belongings.

“We’re out in the boondocks. There's only one road that goes from here to the highway,” she said, snapping her fingers as she formulated a plan. “If they just left, I’m sure we can catch them if we speed. Get in the car and drive.”

Bruce nodded decisively, picking the right key out from his massive key ring. But then as he was walking towards the car, he froze, turning to Wanda with a grimace.

“Ma'am, I, er, don't know how to drive stick,” he said. “Rebecca always did it.”

Wanda could scream. “Oh, for the love of God. Why do I even own a stick shift?”

“Because Rebecca prefers stick,” Bruce added unhelpfully. “And she’s the driver.”

Shaking her head, Wanda stole the keys out of his hand, and jingled them loudly to her audience in the lawn.

“Hello? Ancient vampires? Do any of you know how to drive stick shift?”

She stared daggers at Agatha, then Rio.

“You’ve seen your own credit card bills,” Agatha snorted. “I’m practically a shareholder in the Uber corporation at this point.”

Rio just shrugged. “I can try, if we want. But we might die.”

“Goddamnit,” Wanda sighed. They were all so useless. Including herself. Back when she drove, she’d exclusively used an automatic car. She had no idea what the gears meant.

A warm hand laid over Wanda's, snatching the keys from her.

“Bunch of passenger princesses," Bella said, laughing as she swung open the driver’s door.

***

Agatha, Rio and Bruce were stuffed in the back of the car like three troublesome children, Bruce's hulking frame in the middle so the former two were pushed flush to the windows. They were responsible for keeping an eye out. Officially, that was because Wanda and Bella needed time to strategize; unofficially, it was because Wanda, well…

Maybe if I look at her long enough, I’ll get this feeling out of my system.

Wanda swallowed as she watched Bella’s fingers on the gear stick. The car lurched backward, Bella reversing then quickly accelerating onto the main drag. Soon enough, they were shooting down the street at sixty miles an hour.

She was an impressively good driver.

What the hell can’t she do?

Whatever Wanda had felt that first time, back at Columbia, staring up at Bella from the audience at her lecture—that twisted, desperate pulling in her stomach that she’d referred to as a temporary affliction—it was no longer temporary.

It had multiplied like a virus.

Bella's eyes slid over to her in the rearview mirror.

"Hey," she said quietly, in such a sweet tone that it made Wanda even more frustrated. "It's gonna be okay. We'll find her. My mother is smart, but she’s no genius.”

Wanda’s mouth opened, then closed. Rebecca should be entitled to shoot me for how far she is from my mind right now.

"Yes, right," Wanda said, taking a breath in. She needed a distraction. Anything. "When did you learn to drive stick shift?"

Bella smiled knowingly at the question. “No matter how much I tell you, you’re still so nosy.” But, probably out of pity, she didn’t make Wanda work for it. “I used to do gig work to pay my way through college. Hard to deliver food without a driver’s license. And the cheapest cars were stick shift cars. I drove an old used one.”

"Oh," Wanda said.

She'd forgotten just how little money Bella had when she’d arrived here.

Wanda's eyebrows furrowed. Thinking back to it now—she'd never really gotten that part of the story straight, had she?

“Your family. How did you get away from them in the first place? They seem harder to get rid of than an STD.”

“What gives you that impression?” Bella snorted, but there was a look of quiet anger in her eyes. “It took me two thousand years to get the equivalent of a smoke break from them. And even though I had married and drained enough rich men to pay for two billion Balenciaga bags for my sisters, all my mother decided I was entitled to was a roundtrip cruise ticket from Romania to Boston and some pocket change.”

Her fingers tightened on the steering wheel. “Once I got to Boston, I did as much as I could to completely extricate myself from their life. I didn't use any social media. I didn't want my face showing up anywhere they could find it.”

Wanda remembered how she’d teased Bella about refusing to let her lectures be filmed; she felt a dark pit in her stomach thinking about it now.

“I didn't originally plan to move to New York City,” she breathed. “I wanted to get way farther than that. The plan was Japan. Seriously, you should see my Duolingo level in Japanese.”

Bella’s eyes flitted over to her for just a moment.

“But then I found out about you, and I don't know,” she paused. “The rest felt inevitable.”

Wanda’s heart clenched.

You feel inevitable to me, too.

The thought burnt, but Wanda didn’t try and wipe it from her mind this time.

“Anyway,” Bella said, clearing her throat. “They can’t even be mad at me, because technically my scouting mission went really well. I mean, look at you. I could have been richer than the devil if I had sucked you dry. You should really be thanking me.”

Wanda rolled her eyes, but she couldn’t stop the smile forming on her face. But even though it was clearly a joke—it did make Wanda pause.

Don’t ask her. Seriously, don’t ask—

“Why didn’t you?” she mumbled quietly, so the back row couldn’t hear. “You’re smart. You could have engineered a tranquilizer to knock me out. You could have been the richest vampire on planet Earth. Then you’d have plenty of options for escaping your family, plus more than enough funding for your own research.”

Bella’s smile faltered. She shifted the gear, and the car rocketed up another ten miles an hour. Trees and wide fields of cattle sped by, blurry as oil paintings.

“To be honest, I thought about it,” Bella admitted, then took a breath in. “But every time I pictured going through with it, I…”

She met Wanda’s gaze in the rearview mirror again. Her fingertips trembled on the steering wheel.

“I imagined putting you to sleep, taking all of your money, jetsetting to Paris… to somewhere romantic, and rich, staying at some fancy hotel… eating lobsters, crabs, I don’t know— whatever rich people eat—and then coming back to the hotel room… and finding you sitting there, angrily flipping through channels on the television.”

A fierce blush spread over Bella’s nose. She ducked her head down.

“Turns out, you were the one person I wanted to celebrate my freedom with,” she mumbled. “Which made killing you a bit impractical.”

Wanda’s voice went hoarse.

“Bella…”

A siren wailed just as they were about to curve onto the highway. White lights flashed forward from the exit ramp. Two fire trucks curved around them, headed south.

A fire?

“Those assholes,” Bella cursed.

She jerked the stick, reversing the car into the metal railing separating the road from a steep dropoff. Agatha yelped as the back of the car dented inward.

“Are you trying to skewer us?” she shouted. “I’m a mother. Think of my child.”

Wanda rolled her eyes. “Oh, be quiet—”

The rest of her sentence was locked in her throat as the car lurched forward, turned, then rocketed down the road. Bella looked furious.

Looking through the windshield, Wanda quickly found out why.

A dark plume of smoke crested over the horizon. A few yards out from the main road was a field that was scattered with blackened debris. The lights of what had formerly been the limousine were dangling from a telephone post. Flames were licking across the grass, towards the trees. This would be a full blown forest fire.

Wanda’s stomach was in her throat. No. No. No.

Rebecca had three kids. She knew that much about her.

God, why was that all she knew? Why hadn't she paid more attention?

God, why had she been such an uncaring idiot?

What was she going to tell her children?

As soon as Bella hit the brakes, Wanda tore out of the car, running towards the vehicle. The driver’s seat was sitting in the middle of the field, completely separated from the rest of the car. Sitting inside, one hand still on the steering wheel, was a charred body.

Its eyes were still open.

God, no.

No, no.

She felt the nightmare seeping out again.

“Wanda.” Bella’s arms wrapped around from behind, squeezing her tightly. “Wanda, breathe. It's not Rebecca. Look. Look.”

Bella guided her eyes toward the corpse's face again. The eyes—they were bright blue. Rebecca’s were a dark brown. And this face had facial hair, a mustache now burnt at the edges. The memory came back to her suddenly; she realized she'd seen his face out the window back at the mansion, waiting by the car. He had been their driver.

Her shoulders sagged in relief, but only for a moment.

He still didn't deserve this.

Humans were all worth something, every single one of them. Dignity did not belong to those she deemed important.

She knew what Rebecca would do in this situation. Summoning her strength, she reached out her trembling fingers, and closed his eyelids.

After a moment, she regained her composure, turning away from the body, and toward Bella, who was still holding her in her arms. It took all the strength in her to pry herself out of the embrace, taking a step back. She looked off into the woods.

“Do you think they ran from here?” she asked shakily.

Bella looked at her with concern, but she knew how much Wanda hated being pitied, so she didn’t push the issue.

“Definitely not. My mother probably chartered a helicopter in preparation.” When Wanda looked at her incredulously, she said. “You aren’t the only one with money.”

“I… see.”

“But thankfully, we still have the advantage,” Bella said, taking a deep breath in. “We know what they want, and you know where it is. So all we have to do is get there first.”

Wanda blinked. “Right. Yes… Where it’s… stored.”

“Oh my god.” Bella gave her the most withering look in existence. “Have you ever heard of a single point of failure system?”

Wanda grabbed Bella’s hand, and yanked her driver towards the car. “It’s not single failure. I have one other point.”

***

A powerful, persistent knock thudded on Billy’s door.

He shot into a sitting position, nearly giving himself a heart attack. “What was that?” He yanked the cord on his bedside lamp, and his boyfriend groaned.

“Babe,” Jason said, shielding himself from the light. “It’s like 2 a.m.”

“Yes, precisely,” Billy hissed. “Which is why I need you to grab the baseball bat.”

“Baseball bat? Bill, you are so paranoid.”

“I said grab it.”

Chapter 27

Billy's apartment was different than she remembered it.

For one, all the beautiful Rembrandts she'd given him were nowhere to be seen. They’d been taken down and replaced with “inspirational” phrases.

For example, above the dining room table now hung a “Stay Positive,” and lurking next to the sink was “Be Like Sisyphus” with a stick figure pushing a circle up a hill.

She couldn’t imagine what Sisyphean task Billy was dealing with as a product manager at a dental technology company. But then again, she was just an old hag; maybe selling patient bibs over Zoom was the back-breaking labor of the twenty-first century.

“Mom,” Billy said for the third time in the last five minutes. “What are you doing?”

Wanda picked at the wooden frame encasing the poster.

“Even the wood feels like plastic," she muttered. “Where’d you get this? Temu?”

Billy let out a sigh with enough exasperation to put out a small fire.

"Mother, it is 4 a.m. If you’re here to talk about the structural integrity of my wall-hangings, you’re welcome to come back at any time after eleven."

Wanda crossed her arms, and huffed.

Of course, she wasn’t actually here to discuss his lacking interior design choices. She was here to deal with their very pressing Rebecca issue.

Which she’d been full of urgency and determination to do… before her son had answered the door. Practically naked except for some heart-patterned boxers, and standing next to a young man about his age, who was holding a baseball bat.

Because she’d been right. Billy had a boyfriend, and he had been hiding it from her. But it wasn’t just that. Judging by the little anniversary photo of them at Disney Florida hanging from the fridge, he’d been hiding it for an entire year.

To say it disarmed her a little was an understatement.

Several minutes had passed since in an enduringly awkward silence. The tall, gangly human boy—Jason, apparently—was now standing by the stovetop, staring at the steam wafting out of the tea kettle. Every so often, he’d nervously flit his eyes between Wanda, Billy, and occasionally, Bella.

Bella, who was, yes, also here. Staying completely, uncharacteristically quiet. Of all the times for her to let Wanda take the lead.

Jason cleared his throat. "I think the water’s ready. What kind of tea would you like, Ms. Maximoff, ma’am?"

Wanda blinked at him. It wasn’t that she had been ignoring him completely, but well—yes, she had been. It was just too much all at once to digest. But now he was being all sweet and cordial and obviously nervous, and this was her son’s… boyfriend.

She glanced back at him and tried to smile. "Do you have anything herbal?"

He apprehensively opened the cupboard. From the glimpse she got inside of it, she saw that there was almost nothing in Billy's pantry, save for one box of Earl Grey.

"Ah, no," he whispered back in defeat.

Wanda scoffed, finding another thing to latch onto. She fixed her attention on Billy once more. “Where did all the tea I gave you go?"

Unfortunately, he did not take the bait. "Will you please, for the love of God, tell us why you're here? And who this is?" Billy said, waving his hand towards Bella.

"That’s my…” Wanda trailed off; where was she even to start? “Lab assistant."

Billy shot her a look of disbelief. "And why is your lab assistant here?" He flushed when he made eye contact with Bella. "I'm sorry. You have a name. I'm being rude."

Wanda was reminded eerily of herself when Billy stuck his hand forward and said, "Hi, I'm Billy. My mother is insane, which I'm sure you know, since you came with her."

Bella snorted, eyes bright with delight. It both calmed Wanda and rattled her sails even further seeing Bella look so tenderly at her son.

She had never really imagined them meeting.

She hadn't let herself go that far.

Bella extended a gloved hand and shook his.

"Bella Dragomir," she said. “You have your mom’s curls. They’re lovely.”

Billy flushed, as he always did receiving any compliment. But the name must have jogged something in the back of his mind. His eyebrows shot up, and he whipped his head to the side towards his increasingly nervous boyfriend.

"Ben, oh my god, this is…"

Catching himself, he trailed off, then looked nervously back at Wanda.

What was Billy insinuating?

"Yes? What? She’s my lab assistant," Wanda reiterated in a huff.

Bella laughed, which did not help her case. "Yes. My mother's lab assistant," he finished. "My mother's employee. Anyway, not to be rude, but—"

Wanda flung up her hands. "Why are we here, I know, I know. I’m getting to it.”

She sighed and raked one of Billy's chairs across the linoleum flooring. Joining Bella at the table, she splayed her hands tiredly across its surface, staring at her reflection in the glossy sheen. Her pupils were still blown from the nightmare. She could see the remnants of it pulling at the edges of her vision. They looked like little black whiskers.

Feeling that familiar fear swell up again, she glanced at Bella, who was already staring at her. She was wearing a small, sweet smile, but saying nothing.

I can’t believe she’s immune.

Wanda couldn’t believe a lot of things, frankly.

It had been a long, rainy drive down from upstate. The downpour had left room for a lot of stewing. Wanda had a million things she wanted to say to Bella, but none of them felt possible to vocalize in the company of Agatha and Rio, and certainly not Bruce. And now definitely not in front of Billy and his human boyfriend.

I just need to talk to her in private.

She felt it with a deep, clawing urgency.

As if sensing how unmoored she felt, Bella reached out and squeezed her hand lightly. "I can fill them in?"

Billy’s eyes flitted down to their hands briefly. He gave his mother a loaded glance.

"It's fine,” Wanda said, slowly peeling her hand away, despite how it felt like pulling apart two magnets. “But, Billy, I don't think your boyfriend should be here to listen to this. It's… family business."

Jason put his hands up, already prepared (and perhaps eager) to retreat back to the bedroom, but Billy grabbed his arm and stopped him.

"Jason, stay put,” he huffed. “If your employee can hear it, so can Jason."

Wanda balked. Family business was the phrase they’d always used when she didn't want a human overhearing something vampiric. Had he forgotten?

“Bella already knows about it. So there’s no point in making her leave.”

That seemed to change something. Billy’s eyes widened, and he looked at Bella with a different sort of regard, as if he was appraising her.

“Bella… You told Bella… about family business,” he said slowly. “I can’t believe it.” Wanda frowned. The metaphor was officially running into its limits.

“She’s… well, she’s like family,” she said, trying to guide him to the correct conclusion.

Billy swallowed. He seemed to be summoning the confidence to say something.

“Well, so is Jason, then.”

Wanda paused, her heart thumping against her ribs.

He couldn't possibly mean—

“You found a vampire boyfriend?” she announced, lurching forward out of her chair. “And you hid him from me?!”

“What?” Billy blurted out. “No! How did you just come to that conclusion?”

Wanda reeled, eyes rapidly traveling from Billy to Jason and back again.

“What other conclusion was I supposed to reach?” she hissed.

Billy massaged his temple and dragged his hands down his face before letting out a groan. "Jason is human, mother. He just knows that you're a—"

"Supernatural entity?" Jason cut in with a worried, guilty smile. "Don’t worry, I’m exceptionally good at keeping a secret. My parents still don’t even know I’m gay.”

Wanda just stared, and reeled, absolutely speechless. Eventually, she slumped back down in her chair, falling against it like a dumbbell.

“Oh my god.”

***

Wanda had never intended to tell Billy she was a vampire.

It was a plan which quickly proved unsustainable.

The first time a problem arose, Billy was six years old. The curious little boy had dug his way into her blood supply, and vomited up a gallon’s worth of what he had thought was red Kool- Aid.

She’d told him it was wine, and that kids’ stomachs just couldn’t tolerate the taste. That narrative bought her a few more years, until he was about eleven years old. A worried frown was beginning to permanently etch into his cheeks, and he’d come home from school saying things like: “Other people’s moms eat a meal at dinner time.” and “Other people’s moms don’t have wine every morning and every evening.”

Then, she got a call from the guidance counselor. Your son is worried about you. Should we be worried, too?

She got the picture pretty quickly. Fix your shit, or we call child protective services.

So she sat him down at dinner.

“Billy,” she’d said. “I’m going to give you a word to use to describe me, but it’s not one that I want you to share with your friends, okay?”

She remembered how scared Billy had looked. But he’d nodded, the brave boy.

“You’ve heard of vampires, correct?”

This had been right around the height of Twilight’s fame, which was both fortunate and unfortunate for Wanda. Billy had several—like, three or four—Robert Pattinson posters in his room. And when she asked him about it, he had flushed and said, “I just think he’s a really good actor.” The closet door had always been 180 degrees open.

“Well, Edward is kind of like my distant cousin, in a way. We have some stuff in common, some stuff not. I need blood to live, too. Sun hurts me, although it doesn’t hurt me that much. They greatly exaggerate it. I definitely don’t sparkle. And I sort of have special powers.” She made an abstract gesture with her hands. “But they’re not very special. They’re mostly extremely annoying.”

Billy looked at her like she’d just grown three heads. She remembered how terrified she felt confessing that to him—not only because she knew he’d be skeptical, but also because what if he felt betrayed, terrified, alienated?

What if he saw his own mother as an irredeemable monster?

What if he went to school the next day, and the police were at her door that afternoon?

She could make a lot of problems go away with money. But you can’t buy your way into your child’s heart.

“Is this a joke?” was all he had said in response.

She gave him a tight smile. “No, it’s not a joke.”

She knew any son of hers would require empirical proof. So she had been prepared for this line of questioning. She absolutely avoided using her powers at all costs, especially when Billy was around, but she prepared an illustration of them that protected him.

While her powers were primarily mental, they had a physical manifestation. They typically didn’t injure living creatures, but the reverberations they caused while summoned could impact surroundings—walls, chairs, buildings. It was hard to explain.

So in the spirit of demonstrating, she snatched a newspaper from the foyer, put it on the floor a few feet away, and then told Billy to get behind her.

Then she swallowed, and thought back in time. She didn’t pick out an exact memory. She just yanked at something that felt vaguely painful, like taking a thumbtack out of her foot, and then directed that brief fury at the newspaper.

Black wisps of smoke erupted around it, and then tore it to pieces.

Naturally, Billy had a thousand questions after that. She’d only answered half of them.

From then on, he was introduced to the world of vampires—albeit in a deeply sheltered sort of way. Agatha and Rio flitted in and out of his life, but she forbade them from displaying any of their power to him.

She wanted him to be completely and utterly removed from vampire society. No one was allowed to know about her special boy.

And the most important way to ensure that? Not revealing mommy’s identity to anyone. Because if one human knew, then another human knew. And then eventually it would make its way to a vampire, and Billy would become, at best, bait.

At worst, dead.

All of this lived in Wanda’s mind as she sipped Earl Grey, and gazed at the locked bedroom door. She could hear the loud music that Jason was playing inside, as she’d asked him to do. Billy had been upset with her over her shutting him away for the time being, but then she’d told him it was about Rebecca, and he’d finally complied.

"What do you mean someone has Rebecca?" Billy said. “Like, they just took her?”

Wanda involuntarily glanced in Bella's direction.

Where she had been giggly and spritely before, obviously excited to meet Wanda's son, she was now completely dejected, sinking into her seat like she wanted to be invisible.

Wanda could tell she felt guilty for what happened, but it wasn't her fault.

"You don't need all the details," Wanda said, looking back at Billy. "It's a vampire clan. One that wasn't really on my radar until now. They took her because they're trying to get to the contract. Do you remember what contract I’m talking about?"

Billy’s eyebrows rose. That was thankfully enough to get him off the topic.

"Of course I do. It's only the most terrifying thing Evanorah Harkness ever created,” he muttered. “Aside from Agatha, of course.”

Wanda nodded in agreement.

“And do you still have those… CDs I gave you? Or did you toss them too?"

Wanda tried and failed to keep the hurt out of her voice.

It was stupid. He was a young man discovering his tastes. Even if they were questionable, he'd only had twenty-something years to develop them.

She'd had a thousand years to become this discerning.

But it still hurt. She’d felt excited to give him things for his apartment. To think that they might have been thrown in a landfill, or donated to a thrift shop…

Wanda had never been to Allentown, New Jersey before.

It could be because it looked like every other copy-paste suburb in the Tristate area. A presbyterian church with a white steeple; a few “historic” houses—American historic, which meant they barely qualified to use the term—a pizza place, and a bunch of nostalgic-sounding cafes with names like Petunias and Billy Bobby Coffee.

It was like a frame right out of the video. The Allentown Water Utility building.

The driver stalled the car at the end of a narrow service road. Just like in the footage, a chain- link fence surrounded the property, though several sections sagged inward as if no one had maintained them in years.

That’s the trick. It looks unmaintained. A public building with no funding to demolish it and no use in rebuilding it, it was the perfect specimen.
